# QRE-ENG-A — english-language — SME review sheet (255 Q)

Levels {'L1': 52, 'L2': 73, 'L3': 50, 'L4': 80} · key positions {'C': 64, 'D': 63, 'A': 64, 'B': 64} · microtopics covered 17/17
Status `ai_drafted` / `draft`. ⚠ = verify_fact (statute / rate / threshold — check against current official text).

## ENA-001 · L1 · easy · Explicit detail retrieval · foundation

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

Five years ago, most shopkeepers in Rampur's weekly market dealt only in cash. Today, a laminated QR code hangs beside almost every stall, from the vegetable seller's to that of the man who repairs umbrellas. The change did not arrive because traders suddenly fell in love with technology. It arrived because customers, many of them young workers returning from cities, stopped carrying notes and asked to pay by phone. A trader who refused risked losing a sale to the stall next door.

The benefits are real. Traders no longer need to keep large amounts of change, and the daily trip to deposit cash at the bank has become less frequent. A digital record of sales has also helped some of them obtain small loans, because lenders can now see how much money actually passes through a shop.

Yet the shift is not free of problems. Network failures on crowded market days can leave a payment hanging for minutes, and older traders complain that they cannot always tell whether money has actually reached their accounts. A few have been cheated by customers who show a fake confirmation screen. For these reasons, many shopkeepers still keep a cash box under the counter, treating the phone as a convenient partner rather than a complete replacement.

According to the passage, why did traders in Rampur begin accepting digital payments?

- **A.** Keeping loose change had become too costly for traders  _(error: distortion - reduced need for change is a later benefit, not the reason)_
- **B.** Banks required every trader to display a QR code  _(error: out of scope - no bank requirement is mentioned)_
- **C.** Customers stopped carrying cash and wanted to pay by phone ✅
- **D.** Traders were attracted by the novelty of the technology  _(error: reversed - the passage says the change did not come from traders loving technology)_

**Working**

1. Para 1: 'It arrived because customers ... stopped carrying notes and asked to pay by phone.'

**Formula:** Locate the sentence that answers the question; the key restates it, distractors twist or import facts.  
**Trap:** A benefit listed in para 2 is not the cause given in para 1.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## ENA-002 · L2 · medium · Inference and implied meaning · foundation

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

Five years ago, most shopkeepers in Rampur's weekly market dealt only in cash. Today, a laminated QR code hangs beside almost every stall, from the vegetable seller's to that of the man who repairs umbrellas. The change did not arrive because traders suddenly fell in love with technology. It arrived because customers, many of them young workers returning from cities, stopped carrying notes and asked to pay by phone. A trader who refused risked losing a sale to the stall next door.

The benefits are real. Traders no longer need to keep large amounts of change, and the daily trip to deposit cash at the bank has become less frequent. A digital record of sales has also helped some of them obtain small loans, because lenders can now see how much money actually passes through a shop.

Yet the shift is not free of problems. Network failures on crowded market days can leave a payment hanging for minutes, and older traders complain that they cannot always tell whether money has actually reached their accounts. A few have been cheated by customers who show a fake confirmation screen. For these reasons, many shopkeepers still keep a cash box under the counter, treating the phone as a convenient partner rather than a complete replacement.

Which of the following can be inferred about the small loans mentioned in the passage?

- **A.** Digital payments have made loans cheaper for all traders  _(error: out of scope - loan cost is never discussed; 'all' is extreme)_
- **B.** Only young traders have been able to borrow money  _(error: out of scope - age of borrowers is not linked to loans)_
- **C.** Lenders refuse loans to traders who deal only in cash  _(error: extreme - the passage says records helped some traders, not that cash traders are refused)_
- **D.** Lenders value verifiable information about a shop's turnover ✅

**Working**

1. Loans came 'because lenders can now see how much money actually passes through a shop' - so lenders value verifiable sales data.

**Formula:** An inference must follow necessarily from the text without new assumptions; reject extreme or out-of-scope claims.  
**Trap:** An inference must follow from the stated reason; 'refuse' and 'all' go beyond it.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## ENA-003 · L2 · medium · Main idea and central theme · foundation

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

Five years ago, most shopkeepers in Rampur's weekly market dealt only in cash. Today, a laminated QR code hangs beside almost every stall, from the vegetable seller's to that of the man who repairs umbrellas. The change did not arrive because traders suddenly fell in love with technology. It arrived because customers, many of them young workers returning from cities, stopped carrying notes and asked to pay by phone. A trader who refused risked losing a sale to the stall next door.

The benefits are real. Traders no longer need to keep large amounts of change, and the daily trip to deposit cash at the bank has become less frequent. A digital record of sales has also helped some of them obtain small loans, because lenders can now see how much money actually passes through a shop.

Yet the shift is not free of problems. Network failures on crowded market days can leave a payment hanging for minutes, and older traders complain that they cannot always tell whether money has actually reached their accounts. A few have been cheated by customers who show a fake confirmation screen. For these reasons, many shopkeepers still keep a cash box under the counter, treating the phone as a convenient partner rather than a complete replacement.

Which of the following best expresses the central idea of the passage?

- **A.** Young workers from cities are changing rural shopping habits  _(error: too narrow - only the cause in para 1)_
- **B.** Older traders are being cheated through fake payment screens  _(error: too narrow - one problem from para 3)_
- **C.** Digital payments have spread in the market but have not wholly displaced cash ✅
- **D.** Technology has completely transformed the way small-town markets trade  _(error: extreme - the last paragraph says cash boxes remain)_

**Working**

1. Para 1 describes the spread, para 2 the benefits, para 3 the limits and the continued use of cash: spread without full replacement.

**Formula:** The main idea covers the whole passage including the author's conclusion; reject options that are too narrow, too broad or reversed.  
**Trap:** 'Completely' ignores the closing sentence.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## ENA-004 · L1 · easy · Passage-based synonym and antonym · foundation

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

Five years ago, most shopkeepers in Rampur's weekly market dealt only in cash. Today, a laminated QR code hangs beside almost every stall, from the vegetable seller's to that of the man who repairs umbrellas. The change did not arrive because traders suddenly fell in love with technology. It arrived because customers, many of them young workers returning from cities, stopped carrying notes and asked to pay by phone. A trader who refused risked losing a sale to the stall next door.

The benefits are real. Traders no longer need to keep large amounts of change, and the daily trip to deposit cash at the bank has become less frequent. A digital record of sales has also helped some of them obtain small loans, because lenders can now see how much money actually passes through a shop.

Yet the shift is not free of problems. Network failures on crowded market days can leave a payment hanging for minutes, and older traders complain that they cannot always tell whether money has actually reached their accounts. A few have been cheated by customers who show a fake confirmation screen. For these reasons, many shopkeepers still keep a cash box under the counter, treating the phone as a convenient partner rather than a complete replacement.

Which word is most similar in meaning to 'hanging' as used in the passage ('leave a payment hanging')?

- **A.** completed  _(error: antonym - the payment is stuck, not finished)_
- **B.** dangling  _(error: literal sense - physically suspended, not the contextual sense)_
- **C.** pending ✅
- **D.** drooping  _(error: physical sense - sagging, unrelated to an incomplete payment)_

**Working**

1. A payment left 'hanging' because of network failure is unresolved - pending.

**Formula:** Replace the word with each option in its sentence; keep the sense the passage uses, not a different dictionary sense.  
**Trap:** Do not pick the literal meaning of the word.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## ENA-005 · L2 · medium · Vocabulary from context within a passage · foundation

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

Five years ago, most shopkeepers in Rampur's weekly market dealt only in cash. Today, a laminated QR code hangs beside almost every stall, from the vegetable seller's to that of the man who repairs umbrellas. The change did not arrive because traders suddenly fell in love with technology. It arrived because customers, many of them young workers returning from cities, stopped carrying notes and asked to pay by phone. A trader who refused risked losing a sale to the stall next door.

The benefits are real. Traders no longer need to keep large amounts of change, and the daily trip to deposit cash at the bank has become less frequent. A digital record of sales has also helped some of them obtain small loans, because lenders can now see how much money actually passes through a shop.

Yet the shift is not free of problems. Network failures on crowded market days can leave a payment hanging for minutes, and older traders complain that they cannot always tell whether money has actually reached their accounts. A few have been cheated by customers who show a fake confirmation screen. For these reasons, many shopkeepers still keep a cash box under the counter, treating the phone as a convenient partner rather than a complete replacement.

The phrase 'treating the phone as a convenient partner rather than a complete replacement' means that shopkeepers are

- **A.** using digital payment alongside cash, not instead of it ✅
- **B.** preferring cash and refusing to accept phone payments  _(error: reversed - they do accept phone payments)_
- **C.** sharing their phones with their business partners  _(error: literal misreading of 'partner')_
- **D.** planning to give up cash altogether in the near future  _(error: reversed - the cash box is being kept)_

**Working**

1. The phone works together with (partner) the cash box; it has not replaced cash.

**Formula:** Read two sentences either side; choose the meaning the context forces, not the literal or most common sense.  
**Trap:** 'Partner' is figurative here.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## ENA-006 · L2 · medium · Argument evaluation in a passage · foundation

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

On a summer afternoon, the difference between a tree-lined street and a bare one can be felt through the soles of one's shoes. Surfaces of concrete and asphalt absorb sunlight during the day and release the stored heat slowly after dark, which is why many city neighbourhoods stay uncomfortably warm long after sunset. Trees counter this in two ways. Their canopies shade the ground, and the water that evaporates from their leaves cools the surrounding air, much as sweat cools the skin.

City planners have long treated trees as decoration, to be planted once a road is finished and removed whenever it needs widening. That view is slowly changing. Studies in several cities have found that neighbourhoods with dense tree cover record lower night-time temperatures and fewer heat-related hospital visits than comparable areas without them. Some municipal bodies now count trees as part of their basic infrastructure, alongside drains and streetlights.

Planting saplings, however, is the easy part. A young tree needs regular watering, protection from cattle and space for its roots, and many saplings planted with great publicity die within two years. A city that wants cooler streets must therefore budget for years of care, not just for a single planting drive.

Which of the following, if true, would most strengthen the claim that tree cover reduces heat-related illness?

- **A.** Tree-lined streets are more popular with morning walkers  _(error: irrelevant - popularity says nothing about illness)_
- **B.** Heat-related hospital visits fell where tree cover was added ✅
- **C.** Many saplings die because of cattle and a lack of water  _(error: irrelevant - concerns sapling survival, not illness)_
- **D.** Hospitals in greener areas tend to be better equipped  _(error: weakens - offers another explanation for the difference)_

**Working**

1. A before-and-after fall in hospital visits where trees were added links trees to reduced illness directly.

**Formula:** Isolate the conclusion and its premise; the right option bears directly on the link between them (strengthen / weaken / assume).  
**Trap:** An alternative cause (better hospitals) weakens rather than strengthens.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## ENA-007 · L2 · medium · Inference and implied meaning · foundation

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

On a summer afternoon, the difference between a tree-lined street and a bare one can be felt through the soles of one's shoes. Surfaces of concrete and asphalt absorb sunlight during the day and release the stored heat slowly after dark, which is why many city neighbourhoods stay uncomfortably warm long after sunset. Trees counter this in two ways. Their canopies shade the ground, and the water that evaporates from their leaves cools the surrounding air, much as sweat cools the skin.

City planners have long treated trees as decoration, to be planted once a road is finished and removed whenever it needs widening. That view is slowly changing. Studies in several cities have found that neighbourhoods with dense tree cover record lower night-time temperatures and fewer heat-related hospital visits than comparable areas without them. Some municipal bodies now count trees as part of their basic infrastructure, alongside drains and streetlights.

Planting saplings, however, is the easy part. A young tree needs regular watering, protection from cattle and space for its roots, and many saplings planted with great publicity die within two years. A city that wants cooler streets must therefore budget for years of care, not just for a single planting drive.

It can be inferred that a city which plants many saplings but does not care for them will most likely

- **A.** gain little lasting relief from heat ✅
- **B.** reduce hospital visits within two years  _(error: reversed - the saplings are likely to die within two years)_
- **C.** see night temperatures rise sharply  _(error: extreme - the passage predicts no sharp rise)_
- **D.** have to widen its roads more often  _(error: out of scope - road widening is unrelated to sapling care)_

**Working**

1. Many uncared-for saplings die within two years, so the cooling benefit will not last.

**Formula:** An inference must follow necessarily from the text without new assumptions; reject extreme or out-of-scope claims.  
**Trap:** Do not stretch 'little benefit' into 'sharp rise'.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## ENA-008 · L1 · easy · Main idea and central theme · foundation

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

On a summer afternoon, the difference between a tree-lined street and a bare one can be felt through the soles of one's shoes. Surfaces of concrete and asphalt absorb sunlight during the day and release the stored heat slowly after dark, which is why many city neighbourhoods stay uncomfortably warm long after sunset. Trees counter this in two ways. Their canopies shade the ground, and the water that evaporates from their leaves cools the surrounding air, much as sweat cools the skin.

City planners have long treated trees as decoration, to be planted once a road is finished and removed whenever it needs widening. That view is slowly changing. Studies in several cities have found that neighbourhoods with dense tree cover record lower night-time temperatures and fewer heat-related hospital visits than comparable areas without them. Some municipal bodies now count trees as part of their basic infrastructure, alongside drains and streetlights.

Planting saplings, however, is the easy part. A young tree needs regular watering, protection from cattle and space for its roots, and many saplings planted with great publicity die within two years. A city that wants cooler streets must therefore budget for years of care, not just for a single planting drive.

What is the main idea of the passage?

- **A.** Most saplings planted in cities die within two years  _(error: distortion - the passage says 'many', and this is a detail)_
- **B.** Concrete and asphalt are the main causes of urban heat  _(error: too narrow - only the opening explanation)_
- **C.** Trees cool cities and must be maintained as infrastructure ✅
- **D.** Road widening is the chief reason cities lose their trees  _(error: out of scope - mentioned only in passing)_

**Working**

1. The passage explains how trees cool, reports the shift to seeing them as infrastructure and ends by demanding long-term care.

**Formula:** The main idea covers the whole passage including the author's conclusion; reject options that are too narrow, too broad or reversed.  
**Trap:** A detail from one paragraph is not the main idea.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## ENA-009 · L1 · easy · Passage-based synonym and antonym · foundation

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

On a summer afternoon, the difference between a tree-lined street and a bare one can be felt through the soles of one's shoes. Surfaces of concrete and asphalt absorb sunlight during the day and release the stored heat slowly after dark, which is why many city neighbourhoods stay uncomfortably warm long after sunset. Trees counter this in two ways. Their canopies shade the ground, and the water that evaporates from their leaves cools the surrounding air, much as sweat cools the skin.

City planners have long treated trees as decoration, to be planted once a road is finished and removed whenever it needs widening. That view is slowly changing. Studies in several cities have found that neighbourhoods with dense tree cover record lower night-time temperatures and fewer heat-related hospital visits than comparable areas without them. Some municipal bodies now count trees as part of their basic infrastructure, alongside drains and streetlights.

Planting saplings, however, is the easy part. A young tree needs regular watering, protection from cattle and space for its roots, and many saplings planted with great publicity die within two years. A city that wants cooler streets must therefore budget for years of care, not just for a single planting drive.

Which word is closest in meaning to 'counter' as used in 'Trees counter this in two ways'?

- **A.** offset ✅
- **B.** worsen  _(error: reversed - trees reduce heat)_
- **C.** tally  _(error: wrong sense - 'counter' as something that counts)_
- **D.** imitate  _(error: unrelated meaning)_

**Working**

1. Trees work against (offset) the heat stored by concrete.

**Formula:** Replace the word with each option in its sentence; keep the sense the passage uses, not a different dictionary sense.  
**Trap:** Choose the verb sense 'act against'.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## ENA-010 · L2 · medium · Vocabulary from context within a passage · foundation

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

On a summer afternoon, the difference between a tree-lined street and a bare one can be felt through the soles of one's shoes. Surfaces of concrete and asphalt absorb sunlight during the day and release the stored heat slowly after dark, which is why many city neighbourhoods stay uncomfortably warm long after sunset. Trees counter this in two ways. Their canopies shade the ground, and the water that evaporates from their leaves cools the surrounding air, much as sweat cools the skin.

City planners have long treated trees as decoration, to be planted once a road is finished and removed whenever it needs widening. That view is slowly changing. Studies in several cities have found that neighbourhoods with dense tree cover record lower night-time temperatures and fewer heat-related hospital visits than comparable areas without them. Some municipal bodies now count trees as part of their basic infrastructure, alongside drains and streetlights.

Planting saplings, however, is the easy part. A young tree needs regular watering, protection from cattle and space for its roots, and many saplings planted with great publicity die within two years. A city that wants cooler streets must therefore budget for years of care, not just for a single planting drive.

In the passage, 'basic infrastructure' refers to

- **A.** decorative features added after roads are built  _(error: reversed - that is the old view the passage rejects)_
- **B.** the simplest and cheapest kinds of public works  _(error: misreads 'basic' as 'simple')_
- **C.** essential public services a city must maintain ✅
- **D.** large construction projects such as flyovers  _(error: distortion - the examples are drains and streetlights)_

**Working**

1. Trees are counted 'alongside drains and streetlights' - essential services.

**Formula:** Read two sentences either side; choose the meaning the context forces, not the literal or most common sense.  
**Trap:** 'Basic' here means fundamental, not simple.

## ENA-011 · L3 · hard · Argument evaluation in a passage · foundation

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

For decades, millets such as ragi, jowar and bajra were dismissed as 'coarse grains', food for those who could not afford rice or wheat. Government procurement favoured the two big cereals, and as incomes rose, many families abandoned millets altogether. The result was a diet that grew richer in calories but, in many households, poorer in fibre, iron and other minerals.

Millets deserve a second look. They grow on poor soils, need far less water than paddy and tolerate heat better than wheat, qualities that matter more each year as rainfall becomes less predictable. Nutritionists point out that they release sugar into the blood more slowly than polished rice, which may help people managing diabetes.

The recent enthusiasm for millets, however, carries its own risk. Supermarket shelves now display millet cookies and noodles, often mixed with refined flour and sugar and sold at high prices to urban buyers. Such products may do little for health and even less for the small farmers who grow the grain. If the revival is to mean anything, millets must return to ordinary kitchens as affordable staples, supported by fair procurement prices and inclusion in school meals, rather than remain a fashionable snack for the few.

Which of the following, if true, would most weaken the author's hope that millets can return as affordable staples?

- **A.** Several states have already added millets to school meals  _(error: strengthens - one of the author's proposed supports)_
- **B.** Farmers cannot grow millets profitably at affordable prices ✅
- **C.** Urban buyers happily pay high prices for millet snacks  _(error: does not weaken - consistent with the passage and irrelevant to staples)_
- **D.** Millets need much less water than paddy in most regions  _(error: strengthens - supports the case for millets)_

**Working**

1. If millets cannot be grown profitably at affordable prices, the author's goal of cheap everyday staples becomes unworkable.

**Formula:** Isolate the conclusion and its premise; the right option bears directly on the link between them (strengthen / weaken / assume).  
**Trap:** Options that support the author strengthen, not weaken.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## ENA-012 · L2 · medium · Author tone, attitude and purpose · foundation

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

For decades, millets such as ragi, jowar and bajra were dismissed as 'coarse grains', food for those who could not afford rice or wheat. Government procurement favoured the two big cereals, and as incomes rose, many families abandoned millets altogether. The result was a diet that grew richer in calories but, in many households, poorer in fibre, iron and other minerals.

Millets deserve a second look. They grow on poor soils, need far less water than paddy and tolerate heat better than wheat, qualities that matter more each year as rainfall becomes less predictable. Nutritionists point out that they release sugar into the blood more slowly than polished rice, which may help people managing diabetes.

The recent enthusiasm for millets, however, carries its own risk. Supermarket shelves now display millet cookies and noodles, often mixed with refined flour and sugar and sold at high prices to urban buyers. Such products may do little for health and even less for the small farmers who grow the grain. If the revival is to mean anything, millets must return to ordinary kitchens as affordable staples, supported by fair procurement prices and inclusion in school meals, rather than remain a fashionable snack for the few.

The author's attitude towards the packaged millet cookies and noodles is best described as

- **A.** contemptuous  _(error: extreme - the criticism is measured ('may do little'))_
- **B.** enthusiastic  _(error: reversed - the author doubts their value)_
- **C.** sceptical ✅
- **D.** indifferent  _(error: wrong - the author clearly takes a position)_

**Working**

1. 'Such products may do little for health and even less for the small farmers' - doubtful, not hostile.

**Formula:** Judge tone from the author's own evaluative words and qualifiers, not from views the author merely reports.  
**Trap:** Measured doubt is scepticism, not contempt.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## ENA-013 · L2 · medium · Main idea and central theme · foundation

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

For decades, millets such as ragi, jowar and bajra were dismissed as 'coarse grains', food for those who could not afford rice or wheat. Government procurement favoured the two big cereals, and as incomes rose, many families abandoned millets altogether. The result was a diet that grew richer in calories but, in many households, poorer in fibre, iron and other minerals.

Millets deserve a second look. They grow on poor soils, need far less water than paddy and tolerate heat better than wheat, qualities that matter more each year as rainfall becomes less predictable. Nutritionists point out that they release sugar into the blood more slowly than polished rice, which may help people managing diabetes.

The recent enthusiasm for millets, however, carries its own risk. Supermarket shelves now display millet cookies and noodles, often mixed with refined flour and sugar and sold at high prices to urban buyers. Such products may do little for health and even less for the small farmers who grow the grain. If the revival is to mean anything, millets must return to ordinary kitchens as affordable staples, supported by fair procurement prices and inclusion in school meals, rather than remain a fashionable snack for the few.

Which of the following best states the central idea of the passage?

- **A.** Supermarkets have made millets popular among urban consumers  _(error: partial and not endorsed by the author)_
- **B.** Millets are the best food available for people managing diabetes  _(error: extreme - the passage says they 'may help')_
- **C.** Millets were abandoned because government procurement favoured rice and wheat  _(error: too narrow - only the history in para 1)_
- **D.** Millets merit revival as affordable staples, not as costly fashionable snacks ✅

**Working**

1. The passage argues millets deserve revival but warns against the snack trend; the conclusion calls for affordable staples.

**Formula:** The main idea covers the whole passage including the author's conclusion; reject options that are too narrow, too broad or reversed.

## ENA-016 · L3 · hard · Argument evaluation in a passage · foundation

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

When a group of twelve women in a village first began saving fifty rupees each month, their husbands laughed. Three years later, the same self-help group had lent money to its members for a sewing machine, a buffalo and two children's school fees, and had repaid a bank loan ahead of schedule. Stories like this explain why self-help groups have become one of the most widely praised tools for bringing rural women into the formal financial system.

The model works because it replaces collateral with trust. Members know one another, meet regularly and feel responsible for the group's reputation, so repayment rates are often higher than those of individual borrowers. Banks, in turn, find it cheaper to lend to one group than to twelve separate customers.

Success, however, should not be confused with transformation. Many groups remain stuck at small loans for consumption and never move on to building businesses that could lift their members out of poverty. Training in book-keeping, links to markets and access to larger loans are often missing. Without them, a self-help group can become a useful savings club but little more.

The claim that 'the model works because it replaces collateral with trust' would be most weakened if it were found that

- **A.** close-knit groups repay more reliably than strangers do  _(error: strengthens - supports trust as the cause)_
- **B.** groups of near-strangers repay loans just as reliably ✅
- **C.** many groups spend their loans mainly on consumption  _(error: irrelevant - about loan use, not repayment)_
- **D.** banks find lending to groups cheaper than to individuals  _(error: consistent - this is stated and does not touch the trust claim)_

**Working**

1. If groups without mutual trust repay equally well, trust cannot be what makes the model work.

**Formula:** Isolate the conclusion and its premise; the right option bears directly on the link between them (strengthen / weaken / assume).  
**Trap:** Removing the proposed cause without changing the effect weakens a causal claim.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## ENA-017 · L2 · medium · Author tone, attitude and purpose · foundation

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

When a group of twelve women in a village first began saving fifty rupees each month, their husbands laughed. Three years later, the same self-help group had lent money to its members for a sewing machine, a buffalo and two children's school fees, and had repaid a bank loan ahead of schedule. Stories like this explain why self-help groups have become one of the most widely praised tools for bringing rural women into the formal financial system.

The model works because it replaces collateral with trust. Members know one another, meet regularly and feel responsible for the group's reputation, so repayment rates are often higher than those of individual borrowers. Banks, in turn, find it cheaper to lend to one group than to twelve separate customers.

Success, however, should not be confused with transformation. Many groups remain stuck at small loans for consumption and never move on to building businesses that could lift their members out of poverty. Training in book-keeping, links to markets and access to larger loans are often missing. Without them, a self-help group can become a useful savings club but little more.

The author's attitude towards self-help groups is best described as

- **A.** appreciative but cautionary ✅
- **B.** wholly dismissive  _(error: reversed - the author praises their record)_
- **C.** uncritically celebratory  _(error: ignores the warning in the last paragraph)_
- **D.** detached and uninterested  _(error: wrong - the author evaluates them)_

**Working**

1. Paragraphs 1-2 praise; paragraph 3 warns that success is not transformation.

**Formula:** Judge tone from the author's own evaluative words and qualifiers, not from views the author merely reports.  
**Trap:** Balance of praise and caution rules out one-sided options.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## ENA-019 · L1 · easy · Passage-based synonym and antonym · foundation

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

When a group of twelve women in a village first began saving fifty rupees each month, their husbands laughed. Three years later, the same self-help group had lent money to its members for a sewing machine, a buffalo and two children's school fees, and had repaid a bank loan ahead of schedule. Stories like this explain why self-help groups have become one of the most widely praised tools for bringing rural women into the formal financial system.

The model works because it replaces collateral with trust. Members know one another, meet regularly and feel responsible for the group's reputation, so repayment rates are often higher than those of individual borrowers. Banks, in turn, find it cheaper to lend to one group than to twelve separate customers.

Success, however, should not be confused with transformation. Many groups remain stuck at small loans for consumption and never move on to building businesses that could lift their members out of poverty. Training in book-keeping, links to markets and access to larger loans are often missing. Without them, a self-help group can become a useful savings club but little more.

Which word is most nearly OPPOSITE in meaning to 'praised' as used in the passage?

- **A.** criticised ✅
- **B.** ignored  _(error: absence of attention, not the opposite of praise)_
- **C.** applauded  _(error: synonym, not antonym)_
- **D.** rewarded  _(error: related positive idea, not an opposite)_

**Working**

1. The opposite of speaking well of something is speaking badly of it - criticised.

**Formula:** Replace the word with each option in its sentence; keep the sense the passage uses, not a different dictionary sense.

## ENA-020 · L2 · medium · Vocabulary from context within a passage · foundation

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

When a group of twelve women in a village first began saving fifty rupees each month, their husbands laughed. Three years later, the same self-help group had lent money to its members for a sewing machine, a buffalo and two children's school fees, and had repaid a bank loan ahead of schedule. Stories like this explain why self-help groups have become one of the most widely praised tools for bringing rural women into the formal financial system.

The model works because it replaces collateral with trust. Members know one another, meet regularly and feel responsible for the group's reputation, so repayment rates are often higher than those of individual borrowers. Banks, in turn, find it cheaper to lend to one group than to twelve separate customers.

Success, however, should not be confused with transformation. Many groups remain stuck at small loans for consumption and never move on to building businesses that could lift their members out of poverty. Training in book-keeping, links to markets and access to larger loans are often missing. Without them, a self-help group can become a useful savings club but little more.

The phrase 'stuck at small loans' suggests that many groups are

- **A.** legally barred from taking small loans  _(error: distortion - no legal bar is mentioned)_
- **B.** burdened with small loans they cannot repay  _(error: out of scope - repayment is described as good)_
- **C.** unable to progress beyond small loans ✅
- **D.** content to take small loans purely by choice  _(error: distortion - the passage blames missing training and access)_

**Working**

1. They 'never move on' to building businesses because support is missing - they cannot progress.

**Formula:** Read two sentences either side; choose the meaning the context forces, not the literal or most common sense.  
**Trap:** 'Stuck' implies inability, not choice.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## ENA-021 · L2 · medium · Argument evaluation in a passage · foundation

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

A vaccine is only as good as the journey it takes. Most vaccines must be kept between two and eight degrees Celsius from the moment they leave the factory until they are injected, and even a few hours of heat or freezing can make them useless without any visible change. This unbroken sequence of refrigerated storage and transport is known as the cold chain.

In a large country with patchy electricity, maintaining the cold chain is a formidable task. Vaccines travel from national stores to state and district depots and then, in insulated boxes packed with ice, to village health centres. Health workers record temperatures at every stage, and many storage points now use digital sensors that send alerts when a refrigerator warms up.

Technology helps, but people matter more. A sensor can raise an alarm; someone must still respond to it. A solar refrigerator can work without the grid; someone must still keep its panels clean. Experts who study immunisation programmes often find that failures occur not because equipment is absent but because it is poorly maintained or its warnings ignored. Investing in trained, adequately staffed health workers is therefore as important as buying new machines.

Which of the following, if true, would best support the author's view that people matter more than technology in the cold chain?

- **A.** Digital sensors have become cheaper in recent years  _(error: irrelevant - cost of equipment is not the issue)_
- **B.** Solar refrigerators work well in villages without grid power  _(error: supports technology, not the role of people)_
- **C.** Most spoiled batches came from sites that ignored alarms ✅
- **D.** Some vaccines tolerate short periods outside the cold chain  _(error: out of scope - vaccine properties)_

**Working**

1. If spoilage traces to ignored alarms, human response, not equipment, is decisive.

**Formula:** Isolate the conclusion and its premise; the right option bears directly on the link between them (strengthen / weaken / assume).  
**Trap:** Evidence about equipment performance does not show that people matter more.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## ENA-024 · L2 · medium · Inference and implied meaning · foundation

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

A vaccine is only as good as the journey it takes. Most vaccines must be kept between two and eight degrees Celsius from the moment they leave the factory until they are injected, and even a few hours of heat or freezing can make them useless without any visible change. This unbroken sequence of refrigerated storage and transport is known as the cold chain.

In a large country with patchy electricity, maintaining the cold chain is a formidable task. Vaccines travel from national stores to state and district depots and then, in insulated boxes packed with ice, to village health centres. Health workers record temperatures at every stage, and many storage points now use digital sensors that send alerts when a refrigerator warms up.

Technology helps, but people matter more. A sensor can raise an alarm; someone must still respond to it. A solar refrigerator can work without the grid; someone must still keep its panels clean. Experts who study immunisation programmes often find that failures occur not because equipment is absent but because it is poorly maintained or its warnings ignored. Investing in trained, adequately staffed health workers is therefore as important as buying new machines.

It can be inferred from the passage that buying more solar refrigerators, by itself, would

- **A.** not ensure a reliable cold chain ✅
- **B.** eliminate all vaccine wastage  _(error: extreme)_
- **C.** cause vaccines to freeze more often  _(error: out of scope)_

This unbroken sequence of refrigerated storage and transport is known as the cold chain.

In a large country with patchy electricity, maintaining the cold chain is a formidable task. Vaccines travel from national stores to state and district depots and then, in insulated boxes packed with ice, to village health centres. Health workers record temperatures at every stage, and many storage points now use digital sensors that send alerts when a refrigerator warms up.

Technology helps, but people matter more. A sensor can raise an alarm; someone must still respond to it. A solar refrigerator can work without the grid; someone must still keep its panels clean. - **A.** a power supply that is uneven and unreliable ✅
- **B.** power that is too expensive for villagers  _(error: out of scope - cost is not mentioned)_

## ENA-026 · L3 · hard · Argument evaluation in a passage · foundation

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

In an age when almost any book can be downloaded onto a phone, it is tempting to regard the public library as a relic. Several municipal libraries in Indian cities have seen their budgets cut on precisely this argument, their reading rooms left to gather dust while funds go to more visible projects.

The argument misreads what libraries do. For a student from a crowded one-room home, a library offers what no phone can: a quiet, well-lit place to study for hours. For a job seeker without a printer or reliable internet, it may be the only free place to fill in an online application. For elderly residents living alone, a reading room is also a place to meet others. The books, in other words, are only part of the service.

Some cities have understood this. Libraries that have extended their hours, added computer terminals and set aside space for students preparing for examinations report a steady rise in visitors, many of them young. Their experience suggests that the problem was never that people had stopped needing libraries, but that libraries had stopped offering what people needed.

The author's conclusion in the last sentence would be most weakened if

- **A.** some libraries could not afford computer terminals  _(error: irrelevant - says nothing about demand)_
- **B.** visits fell even at libraries that added the new services ✅
- **C.** e-books have become cheaper than printed books  _(error: irrelevant - does not address what libraries offer)_
- **D.** most visitors to renovated libraries were young students  _(error: consistent - matches the passage)_

**Working**

1. The author concludes that people still need libraries if libraries adapt; falling visits despite adaptation would undercut this.

**Formula:** Isolate the conclusion and its premise; the right option bears directly on the link between them (strengthen / weaken / assume).  
**Trap:** Consistent facts cannot weaken a conclusion.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## ENA-030 · L2 · medium · Main idea and central theme · foundation

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

In an age when almost any book can be downloaded onto a phone, it is tempting to regard the public library as a relic. Several municipal libraries in Indian cities have seen their budgets cut on precisely this argument, their reading rooms left to gather dust while funds go to more visible projects.

The argument misreads what libraries do. For a student from a crowded one-room home, a library offers what no phone can: a quiet, well-lit place to study for hours. For a job seeker without a printer or reliable internet, it may be the only free place to fill in an online application. For elderly residents living alone, a reading room is also a place to meet others. The books, in other words, are only part of the service.

Some cities have understood this. Libraries that have extended their hours, added computer terminals and set aside space for students preparing for examinations report a steady rise in visitors, many of them young. Their experience suggests that the problem was never that people had stopped needing libraries, but that libraries had stopped offering what people needed.

Which of the following best captures the main idea of the passage?

- **A.** Students preparing for examinations are libraries' main users  _(error: distortion - one group among several)_
- **B.** Libraries stay relevant when they adapt to what their users need ✅
- **C.** Municipal budgets should fund libraries instead of other projects  _(error: extreme - not argued)_
- **D.** Digital books have made public libraries largely obsolete  _(error: reversed - the view the author rejects)_

**Working**

1. The passage rejects the 'relic' view and ends by saying libraries lost users only when they stopped offering what people needed.

**Formula:** The main idea covers the whole passage including the author's conclusion; reject options that are too narrow, too broad or reversed.  
**Trap:** The rejected view is not the main idea.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## ENA-031 · L2 · medium · Author tone, attitude and purpose · foundation

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

For farmers who depend on diesel pumps, every irrigation is a cash expense, and a sudden rise in fuel prices can wipe out a season's profit. Solar pumps promise an escape. Once installed, they draw water using free sunlight, cost little to run and produce no smoke. With government subsidies covering a large share of the purchase price, lakhs of farmers have switched in recent years.

The benefits are clear, but so is a hidden danger. A diesel pump's running cost acts as a natural brake: farmers pump only as much water as they must. When pumping becomes almost free, that brake disappears. In regions where groundwater is already falling, cheap solar pumping could hasten the day when wells run dry.

Some states have tried a clever remedy. Farmers are connected to the grid and paid for any surplus electricity their panels generate. Water left in the ground thus becomes money in the bank, and a farmer has a reason to pump only what the crop needs. Such schemes are still young, and their payments must be prompt and fair if farmers are to trust them. But they show that a good technology works best when the incentives around it are designed with equal care.

The author's attitude towards solar pumps is best described as

- **A.** firmly opposed  _(error: reversed - the benefits are called clear)_
- **B.** unreservedly enthusiastic  _(error: ignores the 'hidden danger' the author raises)_
- **C.** wholly indifferent  _(error: wrong - the author evaluates them)_
- **D.** cautiously supportive ✅

**Working**

1. Benefits are 'clear', but a 'hidden danger' needs incentives - support with caution.

**Formula:** Judge tone from the author's own evaluative words and qualifiers, not from views the author merely reports.  
**Trap:** Balance rules out both extremes.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## ENA-033 · L3 · hard · Inference and implied meaning · foundation

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

For farmers who depend on diesel pumps, every irrigation is a cash expense, and a sudden rise in fuel prices can wipe out a season's profit. Solar pumps promise an escape. Once installed, they draw water using free sunlight, cost little to run and produce no smoke. With government subsidies covering a large share of the purchase price, lakhs of farmers have switched in recent years.

The benefits are clear, but so is a hidden danger. A diesel pump's running cost acts as a natural brake: farmers pump only as much water as they must. When pumping becomes almost free, that brake disappears. In regions where groundwater is already falling, cheap solar pumping could hasten the day when wells run dry.

Some states have tried a clever remedy. Farmers are connected to the grid and paid for any surplus electricity their panels generate. Water left in the ground thus becomes money in the bank, and a farmer has a reason to pump only what the crop needs. Such schemes are still young, and their payments must be prompt and fair if farmers are to trust them. But they show that a good technology works best when the incentives around it are designed with equal care.

It can be inferred that grid-connected schemes would fail to save water if

- **A.** payments for surplus electricity were delayed or too small ✅
- **B.** more farmers in the area installed solar panels  _(error: irrelevant - does not remove the incentive)_
- **C.** diesel prices rose sharply in the same season  _(error: irrelevant to the solar incentive)_
- **D.** groundwater levels recovered after good monsoon rains  _(error: out of scope)_

**Working**

1. The scheme saves water only by making unused water valuable; unreliable payments remove that reason ('must be prompt and fair').

**Formula:** An inference must follow necessarily from the text without new assumptions; reject extreme or out-of-scope claims.  
**Trap:** Identify the mechanism, then ask what breaks it.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## ENA-034 · L2 · medium · Main idea and central theme · foundation

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

For farmers who depend on diesel pumps, every irrigation is a cash expense, and a sudden rise in fuel prices can wipe out a season's profit. Solar pumps promise an escape. Once installed, they draw water using free sunlight, cost little to run and produce no smoke. With government subsidies covering a large share of the purchase price, lakhs of farmers have switched in recent years.

The benefits are clear, but so is a hidden danger. A diesel pump's running cost acts as a natural brake: farmers pump only as much water as they must. When pumping becomes almost free, that brake disappears. In regions where groundwater is already falling, cheap solar pumping could hasten the day when wells run dry.

Some states have tried a clever remedy. Farmers are connected to the grid and paid for any surplus electricity their panels generate. Water left in the ground thus becomes money in the bank, and a farmer has a reason to pump only what the crop needs. Such schemes are still young, and their payments must be prompt and fair if farmers are to trust them. But they show that a good technology works best when the incentives around it are designed with equal care.

Which of the following best expresses the main idea of the passage?

- **A.** Solar pumps help farmers but need incentives against overuse ✅
- **B.** Diesel pumps should be banned to protect groundwater  _(error: out of scope and extreme)_
- **C.** Farmers should sell electricity instead of growing crops  _(error: distortion of the grid scheme)_
- **D.** Subsidies have made solar pumps popular with farmers  _(error: too narrow - only para 1)_

**Working**

1. Benefits (para 1), danger of overuse (para 2), incentive-based remedy (para 3).

**Formula:** The main idea covers the whole passage including the author's conclusion; reject options that are too narrow, too broad or reversed.  
**Trap:** The concluding line ties technology to incentives.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

Which word is closest in meaning to 'hasten' as used in the passage?

- **A.** accelerate ✅
- **B.** announce  _(error: unrelated meaning)_
- **C.** postpone  _(error: antonym)_
- **D.** predict  _(error: unrelated meaning)_

## ENA-036 · L3 · hard · Explicit detail retrieval · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

When food prices jump because of a failed monsoon, a central bank that targets inflation faces an awkward choice. Raising interest rates will not make it rain, nor will it bring onions to market any faster. Yet doing nothing carries its own danger. If households come to believe that high prices are here to stay, workers will demand larger wage increases, firms will raise prices pre-emptively, and a temporary shock can harden into persistent inflation. The central bank's task, in other words, is not to fight the shock itself but to prevent it from infecting expectations.

This distinction explains why many economists advise central banks to 'look through' supply shocks that are expected to be short-lived, while standing ready to act if they begin to spread. The advice is sound in principle but difficult in practice. Food and fuel make up a large share of what the typical Indian household spends, far larger than in richer economies. A sustained rise in their prices is therefore not a minor irritation that people can ignore; it shapes their sense of how expensive life has become. What economists label a 'temporary' shock can feel anything but temporary to a family whose grocery bill has risen for three consecutive months.

Critics of inflation targeting go further. They argue that a framework designed for economies where prices are driven mainly by demand is ill-suited to one where supply disruptions are frequent. By raising rates in response to food inflation, they contend, the central bank merely suppresses demand for everything else, slowing growth and employment without touching the original source of the problem. The better remedy, on this view, lies with the government: investment in storage and transport, smoother trade policy and timely release of buffer stocks.

There is truth in this criticism, but it proves less than its proponents suppose. The central bank does not raise rates to lower the price of vegetables; it does so to signal that it will not permit a general rise in prices to take hold. That signal has value precisely because it is credible, and credibility, once squandered, is expensive to rebuild. At the same time, the critics are right that monetary policy is a blunt instrument whose costs fall heavily on borrowers and job seekers. The sensible conclusion is not to abandon inflation targeting but to recognise its limits: supply-side reform by the government and vigilance over expectations by the central bank are complements, not substitutes.

According to the passage, why is a sustained rise in food prices especially significant for Indian households?

- **A.** Interest rates in India are higher than elsewhere  _(error: out of scope - no such comparison is made)_
- **B.** Buffer stocks in India are released too early  _(error: distortion - buffer stocks appear only as a government remedy)_
- **C.** Indian wages are revised every three months  _(error: out of scope - wage revision cycles are not mentioned)_
- **D.** Food and fuel take up a large share of their spending ✅

**Working**

1. Para 2: 'Food and fuel make up a large share of what the typical Indian household spends, far larger than in richer economies.'

**Formula:** Locate the sentence that answers the question; the key restates it, distractors twist or import facts.  
**Trap:** 'Three consecutive months' refers to a grocery bill, not wages.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## ENA-037 · L4 · hard · Inference and implied meaning · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

When food prices jump because of a failed monsoon, a central bank that targets inflation faces an awkward choice. Raising interest rates will not make it rain, nor will it bring onions to market any faster. Yet doing nothing carries its own danger. If households come to believe that high prices are here to stay, workers will demand larger wage increases, firms will raise prices pre-emptively, and a temporary shock can harden into persistent inflation. The central bank's task, in other words, is not to fight the shock itself but to prevent it from infecting expectations.

This distinction explains why many economists advise central banks to 'look through' supply shocks that are expected to be short-lived, while standing ready to act if they begin to spread. The advice is sound in principle but difficult in practice. Food and fuel make up a large share of what the typical Indian household spends, far larger than in richer economies. A sustained rise in their prices is therefore not a minor irritation that people can ignore; it shapes their sense of how expensive life has become. What economists label a 'temporary' shock can feel anything but temporary to a family whose grocery bill has risen for three consecutive months.

Critics of inflation targeting go further. They argue that a framework designed for economies where prices are driven mainly by demand is ill-suited to one where supply disruptions are frequent. By raising rates in response to food inflation, they contend, the central bank merely suppresses demand for everything else, slowing growth and employment without touching the original source of the problem. The better remedy, on this view, lies with the government: investment in storage and transport, smoother trade policy and timely release of buffer stocks.

There is truth in this criticism, but it proves less than its proponents suppose. The central bank does not raise rates to lower the price of vegetables; it does so to signal that it will not permit a general rise in prices to take hold. That signal has value precisely because it is credible, and credibility, once squandered, is expensive to rebuild. At the same time, the critics are right that monetary policy is a blunt instrument whose costs fall heavily on borrowers and job seekers. The sensible conclusion is not to abandon inflation targeting but to recognise its limits: supply-side reform by the government and vigilance over expectations by the central bank are complements, not substitutes.

It can be inferred that the author would regard a central bank that never responded to any supply shock, however far it spread, as

- **A.** certain to push the economy into a deep recession  _(error: extreme and reversed - inaction is not linked to recession)_
- **B.** risking the credibility on which its signals depend ✅
- **C.** faithfully following the advice most economists give  _(error: distortion - that advice includes acting if shocks spread)_
- **D.** correctly leaving all supply problems to the government  _(error: partial - the author calls government action and central-bank vigilance complements)_

**Working**

1. The author says the rate signal matters because it is credible and credibility, once squandered, is costly to rebuild.
2. Never acting even as a shock spreads would squander that credibility.

**Formula:** An inference must follow necessarily from the text without new assumptions; reject extreme or out-of-scope claims.  
**Trap:** 'Look through' is conditional: short-lived shocks only.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## ENA-038 · L4 · hard · Main idea and central theme · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

If households come to believe that high prices are here to stay, workers will demand larger wage increases, firms will raise prices pre-emptively, and a temporary shock can harden into persistent inflation. The advice is sound in principle but difficult in practice. Food and fuel make up a large share of what the typical Indian household spends, far larger than in richer economies. A sustained rise in their prices is therefore not a minor irritation that people can ignore; it shapes their sense of how expensive life has become. What economists label a 'temporary' shock can feel anything but temporary to a family whose grocery bill has risen for three consecutive months.

Critics of inflation targeting go further. They argue that a framework designed for economies where prices are driven mainly by demand is ill-suited to one where supply disruptions are frequent. By raising rates in response to food inflation, they contend, the central bank merely suppresses demand for everything else, slowing growth and employment without touching the original source of the problem. The better remedy, on this view, lies with the government: investment in storage and transport, smoother trade policy and timely release of buffer stocks.

There is truth in this criticism, but it proves less than its proponents suppose. The central bank does not raise rates to lower the price of vegetables; it does so to signal that it will not permit a general rise in prices to take hold. That signal has value precisely because it is credible, and credibility, once squandered, is expensive to rebuild. At the same time, the critics are right that monetary policy is a blunt instrument whose costs fall heavily on borrowers and job seekers. The sensible conclusion is not to abandon inflation targeting but to recognise its limits: supply-side reform by the government and vigilance over expectations by the central bank are complements, not substitutes.

Which of the following best captures the main idea of the passage?

- **A.** Inflation targeting has limits but works best alongside supply-side reform ✅
- **B.** Central banks should raise interest rates whenever food prices rise  _(error: extreme - the author endorses looking through short-lived shocks)_
- **C.** Inflation targeting is unsuited to economies prone to supply shocks  _(error: reversed - this is the critics' view, which the author qualifies)_
- **D.** Governments alone must bear responsibility for controlling food inflation  _(error: extreme - the author calls the roles complements)_

**Working**

1. The final paragraph: 'not to abandon inflation targeting but to recognise its limits ... complements, not substitutes.'

**Formula:** The main idea covers the whole passage including the author's conclusion; reject options that are too narrow, too broad or reversed.  
**Trap:** Do not mistake a reported view (the critics') for the author's conclusion.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

Which word is closest in meaning to 'squandered' as used in the passage?

- **A.** earned  _(error: antonym - gained rather than lost)_
- **B.** measured  _(error: unrelated meaning)_
- **C.** hoarded  _(error: near-opposite - accumulated and kept, not frittered away)_
- **D.** wasted ✅

**Working**

1. Credibility 'once squandered, is expensive to rebuild' - once wasted or thrown away.

**Formula:** Replace the word with each option in its sentence; keep the sense the passage uses, not a different dictionary sense.  
**Trap:** 'Hoarded' shares the idea of handling a resource but reverses its direction.

## ENA-040 · L4 · hard · Vocabulary from context within a passage · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

When food prices jump because of a failed monsoon, a central bank that targets inflation faces an awkward choice. Raising interest rates will not make it rain, nor will it bring onions to market any faster. Yet doing nothing carries its own danger. If households come to believe that high prices are here to stay, workers will demand larger wage increases, firms will raise prices pre-emptively, and a temporary shock can harden into persistent inflation. The central bank's task, in other words, is not to fight the shock itself but to prevent it from infecting expectations.

This distinction explains why many economists advise central banks to 'look through' supply shocks that are expected to be short-lived, while standing ready to act if they begin to spread. The advice is sound in principle but difficult in practice. Food and fuel make up a large share of what the typical Indian household spends, far larger than in richer economies. A sustained rise in their prices is therefore not a minor irritation that people can ignore; it shapes their sense of how expensive life has become. What economists label a 'temporary' shock can feel anything but temporary to a family whose grocery bill has risen for three consecutive months.

Critics of inflation targeting go further. They argue that a framework designed for economies where prices are driven mainly by demand is ill-suited to one where supply disruptions are frequent. By raising rates in response to food inflation, they contend, the central bank merely suppresses demand for everything else, slowing growth and employment without touching the original source of the problem. The better remedy, on this view, lies with the government: investment in storage and transport, smoother trade policy and timely release of buffer stocks.

There is truth in this criticism, but it proves less than its proponents suppose. The central bank does not raise rates to lower the price of vegetables; it does so to signal that it will not permit a general rise in prices to take hold. That signal has value precisely because it is credible, and credibility, once squandered, is expensive to rebuild. At the same time, the critics are right that monetary policy is a blunt instrument whose costs fall heavily on borrowers and job seekers. The sensible conclusion is not to abandon inflation targeting but to recognise its limits: supply-side reform by the government and vigilance over expectations by the central bank are complements, not substitutes.

As used in the passage, to 'look through' supply shocks means to

- **A.** treat them as signs of rising consumer demand  _(error: distortion - supply shocks are contrasted with demand)_
- **B.** examine them closely and in careful detail  _(error: literal sense - 'look through' a document)_
- **C.** refrain from reacting to them if they are expected to pass ✅
- **D.** ignore them permanently, however widely they spread  _(error: extreme - the advice is to act if they spread)_

**Working**

1. The phrase applies to shocks 'expected to be short-lived', 'while standing ready to act if they begin to spread'.

**Formula:** Read two sentences either side; choose the meaning the context forces, not the literal or most common sense.  
**Trap:** The qualifier 'while standing ready to act' rules out 'permanently'.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## ENA-041 · L4 · hard · Argument evaluation in a passage · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

Deposit insurance is one of the quieter successes of modern finance. By promising that depositors will recover their savings up to a fixed limit even if their bank fails, it removes the incentive to rush to withdraw money at the first rumour of trouble. Bank runs, which once turned solvent banks into insolvent ones within days, have become far rarer wherever credible insurance exists. For the small saver, who cannot be expected to study a bank's balance sheet, the guarantee offers a peace of mind that no amount of financial literacy could provide.

The difficulty is that the same guarantee that calms depositors can also make them careless. If savings are safe regardless of how a bank is run, depositors have little reason to prefer a prudent bank to a reckless one, and a reckless bank can attract funds simply by offering a slightly higher interest rate. Economists call this moral hazard: protection against a risk encourages behaviour that makes the risk more likely. Bank managers, knowing that their depositors will not flee, may be tempted to chase riskier loans in search of higher returns.

It would be wrong, however, to conclude that deposit insurance should be scaled back. The alternative, relying on ordinary savers to discipline banks, was tried for much of the nineteenth and early twentieth centuries, and it produced repeated panics in which prudent and reckless banks alike were brought down. Depositors turned out to be poor monitors: they reacted late, then all at once, and often to rumour rather than evidence. Market discipline, in practice, meant stampedes.

The better response is to shift the burden of monitoring from depositors to institutions equipped for it. Supervisors can inspect banks' books, require them to hold capital in proportion to the risks they take, and intervene before losses mount. Insurance premiums can be linked to a bank's risk profile, so that reckless banks pay more for the protection they enjoy. Large, sophisticated depositors, whose balances exceed the insured limit, retain an incentive to scrutinise their banks. Moral hazard, on this view, is not an argument against insurance but a reason to pair it with vigilant supervision. A guarantee without oversight invites abuse; oversight without a guarantee invites panic.

Which of the following, if true, would most weaken the author's claim that depositors are poor monitors of banks?

- **A.** Small savers seldom read the financial statements of banks  _(error: strengthens - supports poor monitoring)_
- **B.** Early runs mostly hit banks later shown to be genuinely unsound ✅
- **C.** Insurance premiums are now linked to banks' risk profiles  _(error: irrelevant - about pricing insurance, not depositors' judgement)_
- **D.** Runs have become rare in countries with credible insurance  _(error: irrelevant - about the effect of insurance, not monitoring skill)_

**Working**

1. The author says panics brought down 'prudent and reckless banks alike'. If runs actually targeted unsound banks, depositors discriminated well.

**Formula:** Isolate the conclusion and its premise; the right option bears directly on the link between them (strengthen / weaken / assume).  
**Trap:** A weakener must attack the specific claim, not the wider topic.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## ENA-042 · L4 · hard · Inference and implied meaning · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

Deposit insurance is one of the quieter successes of modern finance. By promising that depositors will recover their savings up to a fixed limit even if their bank fails, it removes the incentive to rush to withdraw money at the first rumour of trouble. Bank runs, which once turned solvent banks into insolvent ones within days, have become far rarer wherever credible insurance exists. For the small saver, who cannot be expected to study a bank's balance sheet, the guarantee offers a peace of mind that no amount of financial literacy could provide.

The difficulty is that the same guarantee that calms depositors can also make them careless. If savings are safe regardless of how a bank is run, depositors have little reason to prefer a prudent bank to a reckless one, and a reckless bank can attract funds simply by offering a slightly higher interest rate. Economists call this moral hazard: protection against a risk encourages behaviour that makes the risk more likely. Bank managers, knowing that their depositors will not flee, may be tempted to chase riskier loans in search of higher returns.

It would be wrong, however, to conclude that deposit insurance should be scaled back. The alternative, relying on ordinary savers to discipline banks, was tried for much of the nineteenth and early twentieth centuries, and it produced repeated panics in which prudent and reckless banks alike were brought down. Depositors turned out to be poor monitors: they reacted late, then all at once, and often to rumour rather than evidence. Market discipline, in practice, meant stampedes.

The better response is to shift the burden of monitoring from depositors to institutions equipped for it. Supervisors can inspect banks' books, require them to hold capital in proportion to the risks they take, and intervene before losses mount. Insurance premiums can be linked to a bank's risk profile, so that reckless banks pay more for the protection they enjoy. Large, sophisticated depositors, whose balances exceed the insured limit, retain an incentive to scrutinise their banks. Moral hazard, on this view, is not an argument against insurance but a reason to pair it with vigilant supervision. A guarantee without oversight invites abuse; oversight without a guarantee invites panic.

Which of the following can be inferred about depositors whose balances exceed the insured limit?

- **A.** Part of their money is at risk, so they have reason to watch ✅
- **B.** They must pay higher insurance premiums than small savers  _(error: distortion - premiums are paid by banks, linked to risk)_
- **C.** They are the main cause of bank runs in modern economies  _(error: out of scope)_
- **D.** They are fully protected and have no incentive to monitor  _(error: reversed - only balances up to the limit are protected)_

**Working**

1. Insurance covers savings 'up to a fixed limit'; amounts above it are exposed, which is why such depositors 'retain an incentive to scrutinise'.

**Formula:** An inference must follow necessarily from the text without new assumptions; reject extreme or out-of-scope claims.  
**Trap:** Premiums are levied on banks, not on depositors.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

Insurance premiums can be linked to a bank's risk profile, so that reckless banks pay more for the protection they enjoy. Large, sophisticated depositors, whose balances exceed the insured limit, retain an incentive to scrutinise their banks. Moral hazard, on this view, is not an argument against insurance but a reason to pair it with vigilant supervision. A guarantee without oversight invites abuse; oversight without a guarantee invites panic.

Which of the following best expresses the main idea of the passage?

- **A.** Bank runs were common in the nineteenth and early twentieth centuries  _(error: too narrow - historical detail)_
- **B.** Ordinary savers are best placed to discipline badly run banks  _(error: reversed - savers proved poor monitors)_
- **C.** Deposit insurance should be retained but backed by vigilant supervision ✅
- **D.** Deposit insurance encourages reckless banking and should be scaled back  _(error: reversed - the author rejects scaling back)_

**Working**

1. Benefits (para 1), moral hazard (para 2), rejection of scaling back (para 3), pairing with supervision (para 4).

**Formula:** The main idea covers the whole passage including the author's conclusion; reject options that are too narrow, too broad or reversed.  
**Trap:** Moral hazard is a problem the author addresses, not the conclusion.
