Moral hazard, on this view, is not an argument against insurance but a reason to pair it with vigilant supervision. A guarantee without oversight invites abuse; oversight without a guarantee invites panic.

Which word is most nearly OPPOSITE in meaning to 'sophisticated' as used in the passage ('large, sophisticated depositors')?

- **A.** naive ✅
- **B.** refined  _(error: synonym, not antonym)_
- **C.** intricate  _(error: synonym in another sense - 'sophisticated machinery')_
- **D.** wealthy  _(error: associated idea (large depositors), not an opposite)_

**Working**

1. Here 'sophisticated' means worldly and financially knowledgeable; its opposite is naive or inexperienced.

**Formula:** Replace the word with each option in its sentence; keep the sense the passage uses, not a different dictionary sense.  
**Trap:** 'Large' already covers wealth; 'sophisticated' is about knowledge.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## ENA-045 · L4 · hard · Vocabulary from context within a passage · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

Deposit insurance is one of the quieter successes of modern finance. By promising that depositors will recover their savings up to a fixed limit even if their bank fails, it removes the incentive to rush to withdraw money at the first rumour of trouble. Bank runs, which once turned solvent banks into insolvent ones within days, have become far rarer wherever credible insurance exists. For the small saver, who cannot be expected to study a bank's balance sheet, the guarantee offers a peace of mind that no amount of financial literacy could provide.

The difficulty is that the same guarantee that calms depositors can also make them careless. If savings are safe regardless of how a bank is run, depositors have little reason to prefer a prudent bank to a reckless one, and a reckless bank can attract funds simply by offering a slightly higher interest rate. Economists call this moral hazard: protection against a risk encourages behaviour that makes the risk more likely. Bank managers, knowing that their depositors will not flee, may be tempted to chase riskier loans in search of higher returns.

It would be wrong, however, to conclude that deposit insurance should be scaled back. The alternative, relying on ordinary savers to discipline banks, was tried for much of the nineteenth and early twentieth centuries, and it produced repeated panics in which prudent and reckless banks alike were brought down. Depositors turned out to be poor monitors: they reacted late, then all at once, and often to rumour rather than evidence. Market discipline, in practice, meant stampedes.

The better response is to shift the burden of monitoring from depositors to institutions equipped for it. Supervisors can inspect banks' books, require them to hold capital in proportion to the risks they take, and intervene before losses mount. Insurance premiums can be linked to a bank's risk profile, so that reckless banks pay more for the protection they enjoy. Large, sophisticated depositors, whose balances exceed the insured limit, retain an incentive to scrutinise their banks. Moral hazard, on this view, is not an argument against insurance but a reason to pair it with vigilant supervision. A guarantee without oversight invites abuse; oversight without a guarantee invites panic.

What does the author mean by 'Market discipline, in practice, meant stampedes'?

- **A.** strict market rules forced banks to behave with great care  _(error: reversed - discipline failed in practice)_
- **B.** crowds of angry depositors physically damaged bank branches  _(error: literal misreading of 'stampedes')_
- **C.** competition among banks drove interest rates up very quickly  _(error: out of scope)_
- **D.** relying on depositors to police banks caused panicky mass withdrawals ✅

**Working**

1. Depositors 'reacted late, then all at once, and often to rumour' - sudden herd-like withdrawals.

**Formula:** Read two sentences either side; choose the meaning the context forces, not the literal or most common sense.  
**Trap:** 'Stampede' is a metaphor for herd behaviour.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## ENA-046 · L4 · hard · Argument evaluation in a passage · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

Lenders have always tried to predict who will repay. For most of the twentieth century they relied on a narrow set of signals, such as income, employment and past borrowing, which left millions of people without a credit history effectively invisible. A new generation of lenders promises to change this. By analysing thousands of data points, from the regularity of mobile-phone top-ups to the pattern of utility payments, machine-learning models claim to identify creditworthy borrowers whom traditional methods would have turned away.

The promise is genuine, and early results are encouraging. Small traders and young workers with no formal credit record have obtained loans on the strength of their digital footprints, and some lenders report default rates no worse than those on conventional portfolios. For a country in which a large share of adults has never borrowed from a bank, the potential gain in financial inclusion is considerable.

But the enthusiasm deserves scrutiny on at least three counts. First, a model trained on past data inherits the patterns of the past. If certain groups were historically denied credit, the data will contain few examples of them repaying, and the model may learn to treat membership of those groups, or proxies for it such as a residential postcode, as a warning sign. The discrimination is not programmed; it is absorbed. Second, many such models are opaque even to their designers. A borrower refused a loan by a human officer can at least ask why; a borrower refused by an algorithm often receives no intelligible reason and has no obvious way to improve. Third, the appetite for data creates its own risks. Borrowers may consent to sweeping access to their contacts, messages and location without understanding what they have surrendered, and data collected for credit decisions can be misused for aggressive recovery.

None of these concerns is a reason to reject algorithmic lending. Human loan officers have biases of their own, frequently less visible and less measurable than those of a model. The point is rather that the claim of objectivity should not be accepted on trust. Regulators can require lenders to test their models for disparate outcomes across groups, to give applicants a meaningful explanation of adverse decisions and to collect only the data that are genuinely necessary. An algorithm that must justify itself is not thereby made fair, but it is at least made answerable.

The author's statement that 'the discrimination is not programmed; it is absorbed' assumes that

- **A.** machine-learning models are always less fair than human officers  _(error: extreme - contradicts the final paragraph)_
- **B.** historical lending data reflect the past exclusion of certain groups ✅
- **C.** postcodes are the only data point that lenders actually use  _(error: extreme - postcode is one proxy among thousands of data points)_
- **D.** programmers deliberately exclude certain groups from credit  _(error: reversed - 'not programmed')_

**Working**

1. For bias to be 'absorbed' from data, the data themselves must carry the imprint of past denial of credit.

**Formula:** Isolate the conclusion and its premise; the right option bears directly on the link between them (strengthen / weaken / assume).  
**Trap:** An assumption is the unstated premise the claim needs, not a stronger claim.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## ENA-047 · L4 · hard · Author tone, attitude and purpose · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

Lenders have always tried to predict who will repay. A new generation of lenders promises to change this. For a country in which a large share of adults has never borrowed from a bank, the potential gain in financial inclusion is considerable.

But the enthusiasm deserves scrutiny on at least three counts. First, a model trained on past data inherits the patterns of the past. If certain groups were historically denied credit, the data will contain few examples of them repaying, and the model may learn to treat membership of those groups, or proxies for it such as a residential postcode, as a warning sign. A borrower refused a loan by a human officer can at least ask why; a borrower refused by an algorithm often receives no intelligible reason and has no obvious way to improve. Third, the appetite for data creates its own risks. Borrowers may consent to sweeping access to their contacts, messages and location without understanding what they have surrendered, and data collected for credit decisions can be misused for aggressive recovery.

None of these concerns is a reason to reject algorithmic lending. Human loan officers have biases of their own, frequently less visible and less measurable than those of a model. The point is rather that the claim of objectivity should not be accepted on trust. Regulators can require lenders to test their models for disparate outcomes across groups, to give applicants a meaningful explanation of adverse decisions and to collect only the data that are genuinely necessary. An algorithm that must justify itself is not thereby made fair, but it is at least made answerable.

The author's attitude towards algorithmic lending is best described as

- **A.** optimistic and wholly uncritical of it  _(error: ignores the three concerns raised)_
- **B.** receptive but insistent on accountability ✅
- **C.** resigned to its harms as inevitable  _(error: wrong - the author proposes remedies)_
- **D.** hostile and eager to see it prohibited  _(error: reversed - 'none of these concerns is a reason to reject')_

**Working**

1. The author calls the promise 'genuine' yet demands testing, explanation and data limits.

**Formula:** Judge tone from the author's own evaluative words and qualifiers, not from views the author merely reports.  
**Trap:** Proposing remedies is neither hostility nor resignation.

## ENA-048 · L4 · hard · Main idea and central theme · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

Lenders have always tried to predict who will repay. For most of the twentieth century they relied on a narrow set of signals, such as income, employment and past borrowing, which left millions of people without a credit history effectively invisible. A new generation of lenders promises to change this. By analysing thousands of data points, from the regularity of mobile-phone top-ups to the pattern of utility payments, machine-learning models claim to identify creditworthy borrowers whom traditional methods would have turned away.

The promise is genuine, and early results are encouraging. Small traders and young workers with no formal credit record have obtained loans on the strength of their digital footprints, and some lenders report default rates no worse than those on conventional portfolios. For a country in which a large share of adults has never borrowed from a bank, the potential gain in financial inclusion is considerable.

But the enthusiasm deserves scrutiny on at least three counts. First, a model trained on past data inherits the patterns of the past. If certain groups were historically denied credit, the data will contain few examples of them repaying, and the model may learn to treat membership of those groups, or proxies for it such as a residential postcode, as a warning sign. The discrimination is not programmed; it is absorbed. Second, many such models are opaque even to their designers. A borrower refused a loan by a human officer can at least ask why; a borrower refused by an algorithm often receives no intelligible reason and has no obvious way to improve. Third, the appetite for data creates its own risks. Borrowers may consent to sweeping access to their contacts, messages and location without understanding what they have surrendered, and data collected for credit decisions can be misused for aggressive recovery.

None of these concerns is a reason to reject algorithmic lending. Human loan officers have biases of their own, frequently less visible and less measurable than those of a model. The point is rather that the claim of objectivity should not be accepted on trust. Regulators can require lenders to test their models for disparate outcomes across groups, to give applicants a meaningful explanation of adverse decisions and to collect only the data that are genuinely necessary. An algorithm that must justify itself is not thereby made fair, but it is at least made answerable.

Which of the following best states the central idea of the passage?

- **A.** Borrowers should refuse to share personal data with lenders  _(error: out of scope - the author wants limits on collection, not refusal)_
- **B.** Algorithmic lending can widen access to credit but must be made accountable ✅
- **C.** Human loan officers are more biased than algorithms  _(error: distortion - the author says humans have biases too, not that they are worse)_
- **D.** Machine-learning models have eliminated bias from lending decisions  _(error: reversed - bias can be absorbed)_

**Working**

1. Promise (paras 1-2), three concerns (para 3), accountability measures (para 4).

**Formula:** The main idea covers the whole passage including the author's conclusion; reject options that are too narrow, too broad or reversed.  
**Trap:** A comparative claim the author never makes cannot be the main idea.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## ENA-050 · L4 · hard · Vocabulary from context within a passage · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

The point is rather that the claim of objectivity should not be accepted on trust. Regulators can require lenders to test their models for disparate outcomes across groups, to give applicants a meaningful explanation of adverse decisions and to collect only the data that are genuinely necessary. An algorithm that must justify itself is not thereby made fair, but it is at least made answerable.

In the last sentence, 'answerable' means

- **A.** guaranteed to be free from all error  _(error: contradicts 'not thereby made fair')_
- **B.** capable of answering any question put to it  _(error: misreads 'answerable' as 'able to answer')_
- **C.** placed under the ownership of regulators  _(error: distortion)_
- **D.** required to explain its decisions ✅

**Working**

1. An algorithm 'that must justify itself' is accountable - obliged to explain.

**Formula:** Read two sentences either side; choose the meaning the context forces, not the literal or most common sense.  
**Trap:** The author explicitly separates answerable from fair.

## ENA-051 · L4 · hard · Argument evaluation in a passage · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

Economists have long preferred carbon pricing to direct regulation as a means of reducing emissions. Their reasoning is elegant. A tax on carbon, or a cap-and-trade system that sets a limit on total emissions and lets firms trade permits, allows each polluter to decide how much to cut. Firms that can reduce emissions cheaply will do so; those for whom cuts are expensive will pay instead. The total reduction is thereby achieved at the lowest overall cost, without officials needing to know the details of every factory's technology.

Regulation, by contrast, tells firms what to do: install this filter, meet that efficiency standard, phase out this fuel by a given year. Critics see it as clumsy, imposing uniform requirements on firms with very different costs, and prone to capture by the industries it seeks to control. Yet regulation has a record that pricing's admirers sometimes overlook. Vehicle efficiency norms, appliance labelling and bans on ozone-depleting chemicals have delivered large reductions, often faster than predicted and at lower cost than industry warned.

The gap between theory and practice arises partly from politics. A carbon price makes costs visible, at the petrol pump and on the electricity bill, and visible costs provoke resistance. Governments have repeatedly set prices too low to change behaviour, or granted so many exemptions that the scheme became symbolic. Regulations, whose costs are buried in the price of a car or a refrigerator, attract less organised opposition. The economist's instrument is efficient on paper; the regulator's is often more durable in practice.

There is also a question of what prices can and cannot do. A modest carbon tax may persuade a power company to run its gas plant more and its coal plant less, but it is unlikely by itself to trigger the construction of new transmission lines, the development of storage technology or the redesign of cities. Such changes depend on long-term investment, coordination among many actors and, often, public finance. Prices provide a signal; they do not build the infrastructure through which the signal can be acted upon.

The sensible conclusion is not that one instrument should replace the other. A credible carbon price rewards innovation and cheap reductions across the economy; targeted regulation and public investment address the areas where prices move too slowly or where politics prevents them from being set high enough. Treating the two as rivals has wasted years of debate that could have been spent deploying both.

Which of the following, if true, would most weaken the author's claim that regulations 'attract less organised opposition' than carbon prices?

- **A.** Industry lobbies have fought new efficiency standards hard ✅
- **B.** Appliance labelling has cut household electricity use  _(error: irrelevant - about effectiveness, not opposition)_
- **C.** Voters protested loudly when a new fuel tax was imposed  _(error: consistent - supports the contrast about visible prices)_
- **D.** Carbon permits are often traded among large firms  _(error: irrelevant)_

**Working**

1. Organised industry resistance to regulations directly contradicts the claim that their buried costs draw less organised opposition.

**Formula:** Isolate the conclusion and its premise; the right option bears directly on the link between them (strengthen / weaken / assume).  
**Trap:** Evidence of resistance to pricing supports, not weakens, the claim.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

- **A.** respectful but qualified ✅

- **A.** reward the firms that can cut emissions cheaply  _(error: reversed - pricing does reward cheap cuts)_

Vehicle efficiency norms, appliance labelling and bans on ozone-depleting chemicals have delivered large reductions, often faster than predicted and at lower cost than industry warned.

The gap between theory and practice arises partly from politics. A carbon price makes costs visible, at the petrol pump and on the electricity bill, and visible costs provoke resistance. Governments have repeatedly set prices too low to change behaviour, or granted so many exemptions that the scheme became symbolic. Regulations, whose costs are buried in the price of a car or a refrigerator, attract less organised opposition. The economist's instrument is efficient on paper; the regulator's is often more durable in practice.

There is also a question of what prices can and cannot do. A modest carbon tax may persuade a power company to run its gas plant more and its coal plant less, but it is unlikely by itself to trigger the construction of new transmission lines, the development of storage technology or the redesign of cities. Such changes depend on long-term investment, coordination among many actors and, often, public finance. Prices provide a signal; they do not build the infrastructure through which the signal can be acted upon.

The sensible conclusion is not that one instrument should replace the other. A credible carbon price rewards innovation and cheap reductions across the economy; targeted regulation and public investment address the areas where prices move too slowly or where politics prevents them from being set high enough. Treating the two as rivals has wasted years of debate that could have been spent deploying both.

In the phrase 'prone to capture by the industries it seeks to control', 'capture' refers to regulation

- **A.** coming to serve the interests of the firms it regulates ✅
- **B.** being taken over by force during an industrial dispute  _(error: literal sense of seizing)_
- **C.** winning a large share of the market for its products  _(error: wrong sense - market capture)_
- **D.** recording and storing data about industrial output  _(error: wrong sense - data capture)_

**Working**

1. 'Regulatory capture': the regulated industry comes to steer the regulator.

**Formula:** Read two sentences either side; choose the meaning the context forces, not the literal or most common sense.  
**Trap:** The phrase 'industries it seeks to control' signals the regulatory sense.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## ENA-056 · L4 · hard · Argument evaluation in a passage · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

The delivery rider who brings dinner in twenty minutes is, in the eyes of the law in most countries, not an employee. He is a 'partner' or an 'independent contractor', free to log in and out as he pleases and paid by the task. Platform companies defend this arrangement as the source of the flexibility workers value: students can earn between classes, homemakers can work while children are at school, and the unemployed can find income at once, without interviews or paperwork.

There is substance to this defence. Surveys of gig workers consistently find that flexibility ranks among the main reasons they chose the work, and a rigid employment model, with fixed shifts and minimum hours, would suit many of them poorly. But flexibility is not the whole story. Because they are not employees, gig workers generally lack the protections attached to employment: health insurance, paid leave, provident fund contributions and compensation for injuries suffered on the job. A rider injured in a road accident may lose both his income and his savings at once. The risks that a firm would ordinarily bear have been transferred to the individual.

Moreover, the independence is often more nominal than real. Workers who decline too many orders may find their ratings fall and their access to lucrative time slots restricted. Pay rates are set, and frequently revised, by the platform's algorithm, not negotiated. A worker who is free in principle to log off may find that, in practice, doing so costs him the incentives on which his earnings depend.

Recent social-security legislation in India recognises gig and platform workers as a distinct category and envisages welfare schemes financed partly by contributions from platforms. This is a sensible middle path: it neither forces gig work into the mould of traditional employment nor leaves workers wholly unprotected. Its success, however, will depend on details that are yet to be settled: how contributions are calculated, how benefits follow workers who move between platforms, and whether enforcement can keep pace with an industry that changes its practices faster than regulators can study them. Protection that exists on paper but cannot be claimed in practice would merely add one more layer of nominal rights to a workforce that already has too many.

The author's claim that gig workers' independence is 'more nominal than real' would be most strengthened by which finding?

- **A.** Workers who often decline orders earn markedly less over time ✅
- **B.** Many students take up delivery work during their holidays  _(error: irrelevant)_
- **C.** Platforms have recently raised base pay in several cities  _(error: irrelevant)_
- **D.** Most gig workers value the freedom to choose their hours  _(error: irrelevant - about preference, not the reality of freedom)_

**Working**

1. If declining orders carries a clear earnings penalty, the freedom to refuse exists in name only.

**Formula:** Isolate the conclusion and its premise; the right option bears directly on the link between them (strengthen / weaken / assume).  
**Trap:** Workers' preferences do not show whether the freedom is real.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

Protection that exists on paper but cannot be claimed in practice would merely add one more layer of nominal rights to a workforce that already has too many.

The author's view of the recent social-security legislation is best described as

- **A.** unconditionally enthusiastic about it  _(error: ignores the concerns about unsettled details)_
- **B.** hostile because it burdens platforms  _(error: reversed)_
- **C.** approving, but wary of its implementation ✅
- **D.** dismissive of it as a piece of tokenism  _(error: extreme - the author calls it a 'sensible middle path')_

**Trap:** Concern about enforcement is not dismissal.

It can be inferred that the author would consider a welfare scheme for gig workers successful only if

- **A.** it guaranteed every rider a fixed minimum number of hours  _(error: out of scope and contrary to flexibility)_
- **B.** it was financed entirely by government funds  _(error: distortion - platforms contribute)_
- **C.** workers could claim its benefits in practice, across platforms ✅

## ENA-061 · L4 · hard · Argument evaluation in a passage · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

Antibiotics are unusual among medicines in that every use of them diminishes their future value. Each course of treatment kills susceptible bacteria and leaves behind those that happen to resist the drug, which then multiply and spread. Resistance is therefore not a malfunction of antibiotics but a predictable consequence of using them. The question is not whether it will arise but how quickly.

The speed depends heavily on how the drugs are used. In many countries, including India, antibiotics can be bought without prescription at a neighbourhood pharmacy, are routinely prescribed for viral infections they cannot cure, and are fed to poultry in large quantities to speed growth. Each of these practices accelerates resistance while conferring little benefit. Hospitals, where very sick patients receive powerful drugs and infections pass easily from one person to another, act as breeding grounds for the hardiest strains.

It might seem that the solution is simply to develop new antibiotics. But the pipeline has been thinning for decades, and the reason is economic rather than scientific. A pharmaceutical company that invests heavily in a new antibiotic faces a peculiar market: to preserve its effectiveness, doctors will be advised to use it sparingly, as a last resort. A drug that succeeds medically by being held in reserve fails commercially by selling little. Several small firms that brought new antibiotics to market in recent years have gone bankrupt for precisely this reason.

Economists have therefore proposed rewarding antibiotic development in ways that are separated from sales volume. Under one model, governments would pay a developer a fixed annual sum for access to a new drug, regardless of how many doses are used, in the way one pays a subscription for a service. Such schemes treat antibiotics less as ordinary products and more as shared infrastructure, like fire stations, whose value lies partly in being available when needed.

Innovation alone, however, will not suffice if old habits persist. A new drug released into a system of unrestricted sales and careless prescribing will lose its power just as its predecessors did. Stewardship, which means prescribing antibiotics only when needed and in the right dose and duration, must accompany any effort to refill the pipeline, together with better infection control in hospitals. Otherwise, society will be paying ever larger sums to acquire drugs that it then wastes.

The subscription model described in the passage rests on which assumption?

- **A.** Poultry farmers will voluntarily stop using antibiotics  _(error: out of scope)_
- **B.** Fixed payments can make new antibiotics commercially worthwhile ✅
- **C.** Bacteria cannot develop resistance to newly developed drugs  _(error: contradicts the passage)_
- **D.** Doctors will prescribe new antibiotics as widely as possible  _(error: reversed - they are to be used sparingly)_

**Working**

1. The model aims to revive the pipeline; it works only if a volume-independent payment is enough to justify investment.

**Formula:** Isolate the conclusion and its premise; the right option bears directly on the link between them (strengthen / weaken / assume).  
**Trap:** The model separates reward from volume; high prescribing is not assumed.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

- **A.** sensational and alarmist  _(error: extreme - the argument is measured)_

- **A.** their value lies chiefly in the volume of doses sold  _(error: reversed)_
- **B.** they ought to be managed by municipal authorities  _(error: literal extension of the analogy)_

## ENA-065 · L4 · hard · Main idea and central theme · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

Antibiotics are unusual among medicines in that every use of them diminishes their future value. Each course of treatment kills susceptible bacteria and leaves behind those that happen to resist the drug, which then multiply and spread. Resistance is therefore not a malfunction of antibiotics but a predictable consequence of using them. The question is not whether it will arise but how quickly.

The speed depends heavily on how the drugs are used. In many countries, including India, antibiotics can be bought without prescription at a neighbourhood pharmacy, are routinely prescribed for viral infections they cannot cure, and are fed to poultry in large quantities to speed growth. Each of these practices accelerates resistance while conferring little benefit. Hospitals, where very sick patients receive powerful drugs and infections pass easily from one person to another, act as breeding grounds for the hardiest strains.

It might seem that the solution is simply to develop new antibiotics. But the pipeline has been thinning for decades, and the reason is economic rather than scientific. A pharmaceutical company that invests heavily in a new antibiotic faces a peculiar market: to preserve its effectiveness, doctors will be advised to use it sparingly, as a last resort. A drug that succeeds medically by being held in reserve fails commercially by selling little. Several small firms that brought new antibiotics to market in recent years have gone bankrupt for precisely this reason.

Economists have therefore proposed rewarding antibiotic development in ways that are separated from sales volume. Under one model, governments would pay a developer a fixed annual sum for access to a new drug, regardless of how many doses are used, in the way one pays a subscription for a service. Such schemes treat antibiotics less as ordinary products and more as shared infrastructure, like fire stations, whose value lies partly in being available when needed.

Innovation alone, however, will not suffice if old habits persist. A new drug released into a system of unrestricted sales and careless prescribing will lose its power just as its predecessors did. Stewardship, which means prescribing antibiotics only when needed and in the right dose and duration, must accompany any effort to refill the pipeline, together with better infection control in hospitals. Otherwise, society will be paying ever larger sums to acquire drugs that it then wastes.

Which of the following best captures the main idea of the passage?

- **A.** Developing new antibiotics is the only answer to resistance  _(error: reversed - 'innovation alone will not suffice')_
- **B.** Pharmaceutical firms have lost interest in scientific research  _(error: distortion - the problem is economic)_
- **C.** Over-the-counter sales are the chief cause of drug resistance  _(error: too narrow and overstated)_
- **D.** Resistance demands new innovation incentives and prudent use ✅

**Working**

1. Resistance is inevitable (1), speeded by misuse (2), the pipeline fails economically (3), new incentives (4), plus stewardship (5).

**Formula:** The main idea covers the whole passage including the author's conclusion; reject options that are too narrow, too broad or reversed.  
**Trap:** The last paragraph adds stewardship to innovation.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## ENA-066 · L4 · hard · Author tone, attitude and purpose · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

Across much of north-western India, the water table has been falling for decades. In some districts, farmers who once found water at a depth of ten metres now drill beyond a hundred, and the deeper borewells require more powerful and costlier pumps. The immediate cause is not mysterious: more water is being withdrawn from aquifers than rainfall returns to them. The deeper cause lies in a set of policies, each defensible on its own, that together make overuse the rational choice for individual farmers.

The first is the supply of free or nearly free electricity for agriculture, often billed at a flat rate unconnected to the amount consumed. Once a farmer has paid for the connection, each additional hour of pumping costs him nothing. The second is the assured procurement of rice at a guaranteed price, which makes paddy, one of the thirstiest crops, the safest bet in regions whose natural rainfall could never sustain it. Together, these policies convert a scarce resource into a free one and reward the crop that consumes it most.

Proposals to charge farmers for electricity by the unit have met fierce resistance, and not only for reasons of self-interest. Farm incomes in many areas are precarious, and a sudden tariff would fall hardest on small farmers with the least capacity to adjust. Metering is also difficult to enforce in villages where supply is erratic and where the relationship between the electricity board and its customers is marked by mutual distrust.

Some states have experimented with a subtler approach. Rather than charging for power, they pay farmers who use less than a set quota, crediting the savings directly to their bank accounts. The farmer who conserves is rewarded; the one who does not loses nothing he had before. Early evaluations suggest that such schemes can reduce consumption without provoking the backlash that tariffs invite, though their effect depends on the quota being set neither so generously that no one needs to save nor so tightly that few can qualify.

Ultimately, however, no electricity scheme can succeed while procurement policy continues to make paddy the only sensible crop. Diversification towards less water-intensive crops will require assured markets for those crops as well. The aquifer, after all, responds not to the intentions behind a policy but to the total volume of water pumped from it.

The author's attitude towards farmers who resist per-unit electricity tariffs is

- **A.** admiring  _(error: extreme - no praise is offered)_
- **B.** understanding ✅
- **C.** indifferent  _(error: wrong - the author explains their position)_
- **D.** contemptuous  _(error: extreme - the author defends their reasons)_

**Working**

1. 'Not only for reasons of self-interest': incomes are precarious and metering is hard - a sympathetic explanation.

**Formula:** Judge tone from the author's own evaluative words and qualifiers, not from views the author merely reports.  
**Trap:** Explaining a position is not admiring it.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## ENA-067 · L3 · hard · Explicit detail retrieval · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

Across much of north-western India, the water table has been falling for decades. In some districts, farmers who once found water at a depth of ten metres now drill beyond a hundred, and the deeper borewells require more powerful and costlier pumps. The immediate cause is not mysterious: more water is being withdrawn from aquifers than rainfall returns to them. The deeper cause lies in a set of policies, each defensible on its own, that together make overuse the rational choice for individual farmers.

The first is the supply of free or nearly free electricity for agriculture, often billed at a flat rate unconnected to the amount consumed. Once a farmer has paid for the connection, each additional hour of pumping costs him nothing. The second is the assured procurement of rice at a guaranteed price, which makes paddy, one of the thirstiest crops, the safest bet in regions whose natural rainfall could never sustain it. Together, these policies convert a scarce resource into a free one and reward the crop that consumes it most.

Proposals to charge farmers for electricity by the unit have met fierce resistance, and not only for reasons of self-interest. Farm incomes in many areas are precarious, and a sudden tariff would fall hardest on small farmers with the least capacity to adjust. Metering is also difficult to enforce in villages where supply is erratic and where the relationship between the electricity board and its customers is marked by mutual distrust.

Some states have experimented with a subtler approach. Rather than charging for power, they pay farmers who use less than a set quota, crediting the savings directly to their bank accounts. The farmer who conserves is rewarded; the one who does not loses nothing he had before. Early evaluations suggest that such schemes can reduce consumption without provoking the backlash that tariffs invite, though their effect depends on the quota being set neither so generously that no one needs to save nor so tightly that few can qualify.

Ultimately, however, no electricity scheme can succeed while procurement policy continues to make paddy the only sensible crop. Diversification towards less water-intensive crops will require assured markets for those crops as well. The aquifer, after all, responds not to the intentions behind a policy but to the total volume of water pumped from it.

According to the passage, the quota-based scheme works by

- **A.** paying farmers who use less power than an agreed limit ✅
- **B.** cutting power to farmers who exceed a limit  _(error: out of scope)_
- **C.** raising procurement prices for crops other than paddy  _(error: out of scope)_
- **D.** charging farmers for every unit of power consumed  _(error: reversed - that is the tariff approach)_

**Working**

1. 'They pay farmers who use less than a set quota, crediting the savings directly to their bank accounts.'

**Formula:** Locate the sentence that answers the question; the key restates it, distractors twist or import facts.  
**Trap:** Reward, not penalty.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

The farmer who conserves is rewarded; the one who does not loses nothing he had before. Early evaluations suggest that such schemes can reduce consumption without provoking the backlash that tariffs invite, though their effect depends on the quota being set neither so generously that no one needs to save nor so tightly that few can qualify.

Diversification towards less water-intensive crops will require assured markets for those crops as well. It can be inferred that a quota set very generously would

- **A.** make the cultivation of paddy unprofitable  _(error: out of scope)_
- **B.** reward farmers without inducing much conservation ✅
- **C.** provoke strong protests from farmers across the state  _(error: reversed - generous quotas cost farmers nothing)_
- **D.** exclude most farmers from qualifying for payments  _(error: reversed - that is the effect of a tight quota)_

**Working**

1. If the quota is so generous that 'no one needs to save', payments flow without reducing use.

**Formula:** An inference must follow necessarily from the text without new assumptions; reject extreme or out-of-scope claims.  
**Trap:** Generous vs tight: each fails differently.

Together, these policies convert a scarce resource into a free one and reward the crop that consumes it most.

Proposals to charge farmers for electricity by the unit have met fierce resistance, and not only for reasons of self-interest. The farmer who conserves is rewarded; the one who does not loses nothing he had before. Early evaluations suggest that such schemes can reduce consumption without provoking the backlash that tariffs invite, though their effect depends on the quota being set neither so generously that no one needs to save nor so tightly that few can qualify.

Ultimately, however, no electricity scheme can succeed while procurement policy continues to make paddy the only sensible crop. Diversification towards less water-intensive crops will require assured markets for those crops as well. The aquifer, after all, responds not to the intentions behind a policy but to the total volume of water pumped from it.

Which of the following best states the main idea of the passage?

- **A.** Policy makes overpumping rational; power and crop policies must both change ✅
- **B.** Rice should no longer be grown anywhere in north-western India  _(error: extreme)_
- **C.** Farmers deplete groundwater mainly because of ignorance  _(error: reversed - overuse is 'the rational choice')_

## ENA-071 · L4 · hard · Author tone, attitude and purpose · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

Visitors to Indian cities often see informal settlements as evidence of failure: of planning, of governance, of the city itself. The view is understandable but incomplete. For the migrants who build and inhabit them, such settlements are frequently a rational response to a housing market that offers nothing else they can afford. A room in a dense, self-built neighbourhood close to construction sites, markets and factories may be cramped and insecure, but it allows its occupant to live near work, to save on travel and to send money home. The alternative, a formal flat on the city's edge, may be sturdier but is often too expensive and too far away to be useful.

This does not mean that informal settlements should be romanticised. Many lack piped water, sewerage and drainage; they flood in the monsoon and are vulnerable to fire and disease. Residents live with the constant threat of eviction, which discourages them from investing in better homes. The problem, however, lies less in the settlements themselves than in the conditions under which they exist. A family that fears demolition next year will not build a second floor or install a toilet; a family whose tenure is secure frequently will.

Policy has swung between two approaches. The first, clearance and relocation, removes settlements and rehouses residents in apartment blocks, usually on the periphery. Its record is poor. Relocated families often lose their livelihoods, and many sell or rent out their new flats and drift back to informal housing nearer the centre. The second approach, in-situ upgrading, leaves residents where they are and brings services to them, such as paved lanes, water connections and drainage, while granting some form of secure tenure. It is less visible and less photogenic than a new tower block, but it builds on the social and economic networks that residents have already created.

Upgrading is not a panacea. Some settlements occupy land that is genuinely unsafe, such as riverbeds or hillsides prone to landslides, and relocation there may be unavoidable. Upgrading also requires patient negotiation with residents, landowners and multiple agencies, none of whom share the same interests. But the broader lesson is clear: cities that treat the urban poor as a problem to be removed tend to reproduce the very settlements they sought to eliminate, while cities that treat them as residents to be served gradually transform those settlements into ordinary neighbourhoods.

The author's attitude towards the policy of clearance and relocation is

- **A.** neutral  _(error: wrong - the author passes a clear judgement)_
- **B.** vitriolic  _(error: extreme - the criticism is reasoned and allows exceptions)_
- **C.** approving  _(error: reversed - 'Its record is poor')_
- **D.** critical ✅

**Working**

1. 'Its record is poor', followed by evidence of lost livelihoods and return to informal housing.

**Formula:** Judge tone from the author's own evaluative words and qualifiers, not from views the author merely reports.  
**Trap:** Allowing exceptions (unsafe land) shows the criticism is measured.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

- **A.** portrayed as better than they really are ✅

## ENA-076 · L4 · hard · Argument evaluation in a passage · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

When a central bank issues a digital currency, it offers the public something it has not had since the spread of bank deposits: direct access to money that is a liability of the central bank itself. Cash already fits this description, but cash is physical and increasingly inconvenient. A central bank digital currency, or CBDC, would combine the safety of cash with the convenience of electronic payment. Its advocates see it as a natural modernisation of public money in an age when private payment platforms threaten to dominate the exchange of value.

The case is strongest where cash is disappearing fastest. If ordinary citizens can hold only private money, that is, bank deposits and balances with payment companies, the state's role in the payment system shrinks to that of a regulator, and the resilience of the system depends entirely on private firms. A CBDC would preserve a public option, much as a public broadcaster coexists with private channels.

The risks, however, are real and concentrated in the banking system. Deposits are the cheapest and most stable source of funding for commercial banks, and banks use them to make loans. If households could hold a riskless digital currency at the central bank with a few taps on a phone, many might shift their savings out of bank deposits, especially at the first sign of trouble. In calm times, this could raise banks' funding costs and reduce lending. In a crisis, it could turn a slow withdrawal into an instantaneous digital run, faster than any supervisor could respond.

Designers of CBDCs have proposed remedies. Holdings can be capped at a modest amount per person, so that the currency serves for payments but not as a store of large savings. It can be made non-interest-bearing, removing any incentive to prefer it to deposits beyond safety. And it can be distributed through commercial banks rather than directly by the central bank, preserving banks' relationships with their customers. Each of these safeguards, however, dilutes the very features that made the currency attractive in the first place.

Privacy poses a further dilemma. Cash is anonymous; a digital currency need not be. A central bank that can see every transaction possesses a power that citizens in a democracy may reasonably fear, whatever assurances accompany it. Some designs allow anonymity for small payments while retaining traceability for large ones, an attempt to balance privacy against the prevention of crime. How that balance is struck may do more than any technical feature to determine whether the public trusts the new money.

The author's warning that a CBDC could cause an 'instantaneous digital run' depends on which assumption?

- **A.** Cash would be abolished once a CBDC is introduced  _(error: out of scope)_
- **B.** The central bank would pay high interest on CBDC holdings  _(error: not needed - safety alone drives the shift)_
- **C.** Savers would switch to a CBDC fast if banks looked unsafe ✅
- **D.** Commercial banks would refuse to distribute the CBDC  _(error: out of scope)_

**Working**

1. A run requires depositors to move money into the riskless CBDC 'at the first sign of trouble'.

**Formula:** Isolate the conclusion and its premise; the right option bears directly on the link between them (strengthen / weaken / assume).  
**Trap:** Interest is discussed as a remedy, not a precondition of the run.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

If ordinary citizens can hold only private money, that is, bank deposits and balances with payment companies, the state's role in the payment system shrinks to that of a regulator, and the resilience of the system depends entirely on private firms. A CBDC would preserve a public option, much as a public broadcaster coexists with private channels.

The risks, however, are real and concentrated in the banking system. Deposits are the cheapest and most stable source of funding for commercial banks, and banks use them to make loans. If households could hold a riskless digital currency at the central bank with a few taps on a phone, many might shift their savings out of bank deposits, especially at the first sign of trouble. In calm times, this could raise banks' funding costs and reduce lending. In a crisis, it could turn a slow withdrawal into an instantaneous digital run, faster than any supervisor could respond.

Designers of CBDCs have proposed remedies. Holdings can be capped at a modest amount per person, so that the currency serves for payments but not as a store of large savings. It can be made non-interest-bearing, removing any incentive to prefer it to deposits beyond safety. And it can be distributed through commercial banks rather than directly by the central bank, preserving banks' relationships with their customers. Each of these safeguards, however, dilutes the very features that made the currency attractive in the first place.

Privacy poses a further dilemma. Cash is anonymous; a digital currency need not be. A central bank that can see every transaction possesses a power that citizens in a democracy may reasonably fear, whatever assurances accompany it. Some designs allow anonymity for small payments while retaining traceability for large ones, an attempt to balance privacy against the prevention of crime. How that balance is struck may do more than any technical feature to determine whether the public trusts the new money.

Which of the following is NOT proposed in the passage as a safeguard for banks?

- **A.** distributing it through commercial banks  _(error: proposed)_
- **B.** capping holdings per person  _(error: proposed)_
- **C.** charging a fee on every CBDC payment ✅
- **D.** paying no interest on holdings  _(error: proposed)_

**Working**

1. Para 4 lists caps, zero interest and distribution through banks; transaction fees are never mentioned.

**Formula:** Locate the sentence that answers the question; the key restates it, distractors twist or import facts.  
**Trap:** NOT questions: tick off each listed item.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## ENA-078 · L4 · hard · Inference and implied meaning · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

When a central bank issues a digital currency, it offers the public something it has not had since the spread of bank deposits: direct access to money that is a liability of the central bank itself. Cash already fits this description, but cash is physical and increasingly inconvenient. A central bank digital currency, or CBDC, would combine the safety of cash with the convenience of electronic payment. Its advocates see it as a natural modernisation of public money in an age when private payment platforms threaten to dominate the exchange of value.

The case is strongest where cash is disappearing fastest. If ordinary citizens can hold only private money, that is, bank deposits and balances with payment companies, the state's role in the payment system shrinks to that of a regulator, and the resilience of the system depends entirely on private firms. A CBDC would preserve a public option, much as a public broadcaster coexists with private channels.

The risks, however, are real and concentrated in the banking system. Deposits are the cheapest and most stable source of funding for commercial banks, and banks use them to make loans. If households could hold a riskless digital currency at the central bank with a few taps on a phone, many might shift their savings out of bank deposits, especially at the first sign of trouble. In calm times, this could raise banks' funding costs and reduce lending. In a crisis, it could turn a slow withdrawal into an instantaneous digital run, faster than any supervisor could respond.

It can be made non-interest-bearing, removing any incentive to prefer it to deposits beyond safety. And it can be distributed through commercial banks rather than directly by the central bank, preserving banks' relationships with their customers. Each of these safeguards, however, dilutes the very features that made the currency attractive in the first place.

Privacy poses a further dilemma. Cash is anonymous; a digital currency need not be. A central bank that can see every transaction possesses a power that citizens in a democracy may reasonably fear, whatever assurances accompany it. Some designs allow anonymity for small payments while retaining traceability for large ones, an attempt to balance privacy against the prevention of crime. How that balance is struck may do more than any technical feature to determine whether the public trusts the new money.

The author's comparison with a public broadcaster suggests that a CBDC is meant to

- **A.** replace private payment platforms entirely over time  _(error: extreme - 'coexists')_
- **B.** operate on a commercial, profit-seeking basis like a firm  _(error: reversed - a public option)_
- **C.** broadcast information about monetary policy to the public  _(error: literal extension of the analogy)_
- **D.** offer a public alternative alongside private payment options ✅

**Working**

1. A public broadcaster 'coexists with private channels'; likewise a CBDC would 'preserve a public option'.

**Formula:** An inference must follow necessarily from the text without new assumptions; reject extreme or out-of-scope claims.  
**Trap:** The key word of the analogy is 'coexists'.

In the passage, money that is 'a liability of the central bank' is best understood as

- **A.** a legal fault for which the bank may be sued  _(error: wrong sense - legal liability)_
- **B.** a claim the central bank owes to the holder ✅
- **C.** an asset held in the central bank's reserves  _(error: reversed - an asset of the holder, a liability of the issuer)_
- **D.** a burden or disadvantage for the central bank  _(error: general sense of 'liability', not the financial one)_

**Working**

1. Cash 'already fits this description': it is the central bank's obligation to the holder.

**Formula:** Read two sentences either side; choose the meaning the context forces, not the literal or most common sense.

## ENA-081 · L4 · hard · Argument evaluation in a passage · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

Science is often described as self-correcting, and in the long run it usually is. But the phrase can conceal how slowly and unevenly correction occurs. Over the past two decades, systematic attempts to repeat published experiments in psychology, medicine and other fields have found that a substantial share of results could not be reproduced. Findings that had been cited hundreds of times, taught in textbooks and used to shape policy turned out, on closer inspection, to rest on weak foundations.

The causes are rarely fraud. More often they lie in the incentives under which research is produced. Journals prefer striking, positive findings to null results; careers depend on publishing in such journals; and researchers enjoy considerable freedom in how they collect and analyse data. A scientist who tries several analyses and reports only the one that produces a significant result need not be dishonest in any deliberate sense. He may sincerely believe he has found the best way to look at the data. But the practice, repeated across thousands of studies, fills the literature with results that are more likely to be flukes than their reported statistics suggest.

The remedies proposed are, for the most part, procedural. Pre-registration requires researchers to state their hypotheses and methods before collecting data, so that later analytical choices cannot be tailored to the results. Registered reports go further: journals agree to publish a study on the basis of its design, before the results are known, removing the premium on positive findings. Data-sharing allows others to check analyses independently, and dedicated replication studies, once regarded as unoriginal and hard to publish, are gaining respectability.

Sceptics warn that these reforms risk stifling exploratory research, in which scientists follow unexpected leads without a fixed plan. The concern is legitimate but overstated. Pre-registration does not forbid exploration; it asks only that exploratory findings be labelled as such, rather than presented as tests of hypotheses formed in advance. The distinction matters because an exploratory finding is a reason for further study, not a conclusion.

What the crisis reveals, ultimately, is that the reliability of science is not guaranteed by the character of individual scientists but by the design of the institutions within which they work. Honest people responding to distorted incentives can collectively produce an unreliable literature. Correcting that requires changing the incentives, not merely exhorting researchers to be more careful.

Which of the following, if true, would most undermine the author's claim that the causes of irreproducibility are 'rarely fraud'?

- **A.** Replication studies have become easier to publish  _(error: irrelevant)_
- **B.** Audits find most irreproducible studies involved fabricated data ✅
- **C.** Researchers often try several analyses before reporting one  _(error: supports the incentives explanation)_
- **D.** Journals increasingly accept registered reports  _(error: irrelevant - about remedies)_

**Working**

1. Direct evidence that fabrication explains most failures contradicts 'rarely fraud'.

**Formula:** Isolate the conclusion and its premise; the right option bears directly on the link between them (strengthen / weaken / assume).  
**Trap:** Evidence for the author's alternative cause strengthens, not undermines.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

The distinction matters because an exploratory finding is a reason for further study, not a conclusion.

Honest people responding to distorted incentives can collectively produce an unreliable literature. Correcting that requires changing the incentives, not merely exhorting researchers to be more careful.

The author's response to the sceptics of reform can best be described as

- **A.** measured disagreement ✅
- **B.** complete agreement  _(error: reversed - 'overstated')_
- **C.** contemptuous dismissal  _(error: extreme - the concern is called 'legitimate')_
- **D.** anxious uncertainty  _(error: wrong - the author answers confidently)_

**Working**

1. 'The concern is legitimate but overstated' - partial concession, then rebuttal.

**Formula:** Judge tone from the author's own evaluative words and qualifiers, not from views the author merely reports.  
**Trap:** 'Legitimate' rules out contempt.

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

## ENA-084 · L4 · hard · Main idea and central theme · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

Science is often described as self-correcting, and in the long run it usually is. But the phrase can conceal how slowly and unevenly correction occurs. Over the past two decades, systematic attempts to repeat published experiments in psychology, medicine and other fields have found that a substantial share of results could not be reproduced. Findings that had been cited hundreds of times, taught in textbooks and used to shape policy turned out, on closer inspection, to rest on weak foundations.

The causes are rarely fraud. More often they lie in the incentives under which research is produced. Journals prefer striking, positive findings to null results; careers depend on publishing in such journals; and researchers enjoy considerable freedom in how they collect and analyse data. A scientist who tries several analyses and reports only the one that produces a significant result need not be dishonest in any deliberate sense. He may sincerely believe he has found the best way to look at the data. But the practice, repeated across thousands of studies, fills the literature with results that are more likely to be flukes than their reported statistics suggest.

The remedies proposed are, for the most part, procedural. Pre-registration requires researchers to state their hypotheses and methods before collecting data, so that later analytical choices cannot be tailored to the results. Registered reports go further: journals agree to publish a study on the basis of its design, before the results are known, removing the premium on positive findings. Data-sharing allows others to check analyses independently, and dedicated replication studies, once regarded as unoriginal and hard to publish, are gaining respectability.

Sceptics warn that these reforms risk stifling exploratory research, in which scientists follow unexpected leads without a fixed plan. The concern is legitimate but overstated. Pre-registration does not forbid exploration; it asks only that exploratory findings be labelled as such, rather than presented as tests of hypotheses formed in advance. The distinction matters because an exploratory finding is a reason for further study, not a conclusion.

What the crisis reveals, ultimately, is that the reliability of science is not guaranteed by the character of individual scientists but by the design of the institutions within which they work. Honest people responding to distorted incentives can collectively produce an unreliable literature. Correcting that requires changing the incentives, not merely exhorting researchers to be more careful.

Which of the following best captures the main idea of the passage?

- **A.** Pre-registration will end all exploratory scientific research  _(error: distortion of the sceptics' view, which the author rejects)_
- **B.** Science has stopped being self-correcting in recent decades  _(error: extreme - 'in the long run it usually is')_
- **C.** Most published scientific findings are the product of fraud  _(error: reversed)_
- **D.** Irreproducible science stems from incentives that need reform ✅

**Working**

1. Crisis (1), incentives not fraud (2), procedural remedies (3-4), institutions over individuals (5).

**Formula:** The main idea covers the whole passage including the author's conclusion; reject options that are too narrow, too broad or reversed.  
**Trap:** The concluding paragraph states the thesis.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

- **C.** results of great importance  _(error: unrelated sense)_
- **D.** results produced by mere chance ✅

## ENA-086 · L4 · hard · Argument evaluation in a passage · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

Economists have long observed a puzzling gap between the visible spread of new technologies and their measured effect on productivity. Offices filled with computers in the 1970s and 1980s, yet output per worker in many advanced economies grew more slowly than in the preceding decades. A similar puzzle has accompanied the smartphone, cloud computing and, most recently, artificial intelligence: the technology seems ubiquitous, but the productivity gains remain elusive.

Several explanations compete. The first is mismeasurement. Much of the value of digital technology takes forms that conventional statistics struggle to capture, such as free services, improvements in quality and time saved, and national accounts built for an economy of physical goods may undercount it. The second explanation is that the new technologies are simply less transformative than their predecessors: a search engine, however useful, may matter less to living standards than electricity or indoor plumbing did.

A third explanation, and the one with the strongest historical support, emphasises delay. General-purpose technologies, those which, like electricity, eventually reshape almost every industry, rarely raise productivity as soon as they appear. Factories that first adopted electric motors simply replaced their central steam engines with a single large motor and kept the old layout. Only decades later, when a new generation of managers redesigned factories around small motors attached to individual machines, did the full benefit arrive. The technology had been available for years; what was missing was the reorganisation of work around it.

If this account is right, the productivity of a new technology depends less on the technology itself than on complementary investments: in training, in management practices, in the redesign of processes and organisations. These investments are costly, slow and often invisible in the statistics. A firm that spends two years retraining staff and rebuilding its workflows may appear, during that period, to have become less productive, even as it lays the ground for large future gains.

The lesson for policy is not to wait passively for the gains to appear. Governments can accelerate the diffusion of complementary skills through education, support smaller firms that lack the resources to reorganise, and avoid regulations that lock in old ways of working. Technology offers a possibility; whether and when that possibility becomes prosperity depends on choices made by people and institutions.

Which of the following, if true, would most strengthen the 'delay' explanation discussed in the passage?

- **A.** Electricity raised living standards more than search engines did  _(error: supports the 'less transformative' explanation)_
- **B.** Free digital services are not counted in national accounts  _(error: supports mismeasurement, a rival explanation)_
- **C.** Firms that retrained staff after adopting AI later outperformed peers ✅
- **D.** Many firms adopted computers during the 1970s and 1980s  _(error: restates background, no support)_

**Working**

1. The delay view says gains follow complementary investment; evidence that retrainers later outperformed fits exactly.

**Formula:** Isolate the conclusion and its premise; the right option bears directly on the link between them (strengthen / weaken / assume).  
**Trap:** Match the evidence to the specific explanation.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## ENA-088 · L3 · hard · Explicit detail retrieval · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

Economists have long observed a puzzling gap between the visible spread of new technologies and their measured effect on productivity. Offices filled with computers in the 1970s and 1980s, yet output per worker in many advanced economies grew more slowly than in the preceding decades. A similar puzzle has accompanied the smartphone, cloud computing and, most recently, artificial intelligence: the technology seems ubiquitous, but the productivity gains remain elusive.

Several explanations compete. The first is mismeasurement. Much of the value of digital technology takes forms that conventional statistics struggle to capture, such as free services, improvements in quality and time saved, and national accounts built for an economy of physical goods may undercount it. The second explanation is that the new technologies are simply less transformative than their predecessors: a search engine, however useful, may matter less to living standards than electricity or indoor plumbing did.

A third explanation, and the one with the strongest historical support, emphasises delay. General-purpose technologies, those which, like electricity, eventually reshape almost every industry, rarely raise productivity as soon as they appear. Factories that first adopted electric motors simply replaced their central steam engines with a single large motor and kept the old layout. Only decades later, when a new generation of managers redesigned factories around small motors attached to individual machines, did the full benefit arrive. The technology had been available for years; what was missing was the reorganisation of work around it.

If this account is right, the productivity of a new technology depends less on the technology itself than on complementary investments: in training, in management practices, in the redesign of processes and organisations. These investments are costly, slow and often invisible in the statistics. A firm that spends two years retraining staff and rebuilding its workflows may appear, during that period, to have become less productive, even as it lays the ground for large future gains.

The lesson for policy is not to wait passively for the gains to appear. Governments can accelerate the diffusion of complementary skills through education, support smaller firms that lack the resources to reorganise, and avoid regulations that lock in old ways of working. Technology offers a possibility; whether and when that possibility becomes prosperity depends on choices made by people and institutions.

According to the passage, the factories that first adopted electric motors

- **A.** replaced steam engines but kept the old factory layout ✅
- **B.** abandoned electricity for steam after a few years  _(error: out of scope)_
- **C.** saw immediate and large rises in their output  _(error: reversed)_
- **D.** attached small motors to individual machines at once  _(error: reversed - that came decades later)_

**Working**

1. '... simply replaced their central steam engines with a single large motor and kept the old layout.'

**Formula:** Locate the sentence that answers the question; the key restates it, distractors twist or import facts.  
**Trap:** Watch the sequence: redesign came later.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## ENA-089 · L4 · hard · Main idea and central theme · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

These investments are costly, slow and often invisible in the statistics. A firm that spends two years retraining staff and rebuilding its workflows may appear, during that period, to have become less productive, even as it lays the ground for large future gains.

Governments can accelerate the diffusion of complementary skills through education, support smaller firms that lack the resources to reorganise, and avoid regulations that lock in old ways of working. Technology offers a possibility; whether and when that possibility becomes prosperity depends on choices made by people and institutions.

Which of the following best states the main idea of the passage?

- **A.** Governments should slow the adoption of new technologies  _(error: reversed - the author urges acceleration of complements)_
- **B.** Productivity statistics fail to capture the value of digital technology  _(error: one rival explanation, not the author's thesis)_
- **C.** Recent technologies are less transformative than electricity was  _(error: another rival explanation)_
- **D.** New technologies raise productivity only after complementary reorganisation ✅

**Working**

1. The author favours the delay explanation, develops complementary investments and draws policy lessons from it.

**Formula:** The main idea covers the whole passage including the author's conclusion; reject options that are too narrow, too broad or reversed.  
**Trap:** The explanation with 'the strongest historical support' drives the rest.

## ENA-091 · L4 · hard · Author tone, attitude and purpose · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

For most of the twentieth century, mangrove forests along India's coasts were regarded as wasteland, muddy, mosquito-ridden and unproductive, to be cleared for aquaculture ponds, ports or housing. That judgement has been overturned by a growing body of evidence showing that mangroves perform services which engineered structures replicate only at great cost. Their dense roots trap sediment, slow tidal currents and absorb the energy of waves, protecting the land behind them from erosion and storm surges. After cyclones, villages sheltered by intact mangroves have repeatedly suffered less damage than neighbouring villages whose forests had been cleared.

The protective function is only part of the value. Mangroves serve as nurseries for many species of fish and crustaceans that support coastal fisheries, and their waterlogged soils store carbon at rates per hectare that exceed those of many inland forests. A hectare cleared for a shrimp farm may yield a quick return, but it sacrifices a stream of benefits that accrue, often unnoticed, to fishers, farmers and the wider public.

This mismatch between private gain and public loss explains much of the destruction. The benefits of clearing are captured by a single owner and appear in the accounts; the costs are dispersed among thousands of people and appear nowhere. Economists describe mangroves as a classic case of undervalued natural capital, and efforts to estimate their worth in monetary terms, though imperfect, have helped persuade governments to take them seriously.

Restoration has accordingly become popular, but it is not as simple as planting seedlings in the mud. Many past plantation drives failed because saplings of a single species were planted in the wrong zone, where tidal flooding was too frequent or too rare for them to survive. Successful projects begin by restoring the natural flow of water that the forest needs, often by breaching old embankments, and then allow mangroves to regenerate, supplementing with planting only where necessary. Such projects are less dramatic than mass planting ceremonies and yield fewer photographs, but they produce forests that last.

Equally important is the involvement of local communities. Where residents have secure rights to use mangrove resources sustainably, they have often proved the most effective guardians against illegal cutting. Where they are excluded, the forest becomes, in effect, no one's responsibility. Protection, like restoration, works best when those who live beside the forest have a stake in its survival.

The author's attitude towards mass mangrove-planting ceremonies is

- **A.** openly hostile  _(error: extreme - the author only notes they yield fewer lasting forests)_
- **B.** wholeheartedly supportive  _(error: reversed)_
- **C.** mildly sceptical ✅
- **D.** entirely indifferent  _(error: wrong - the author contrasts them unfavourably)_

**Working**

1. Hydrology-led projects are 'less dramatic ... fewer photographs, but they produce forests that last' - an implied doubt about ceremonies.

**Formula:** Judge tone from the author's own evaluative words and qualifiers, not from views the author merely reports.  
**Trap:** An unfavourable comparison is not hostility.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

Many past plantation drives failed because saplings of a single species were planted in the wrong zone, where tidal flooding was too frequent or too rare for them to survive. Successful projects begin by restoring the natural flow of water that the forest needs, often by breaching old embankments, and then allow mangroves to regenerate, supplementing with planting only where necessary. Such projects are less dramatic than mass planting ceremonies and yield fewer photographs, but they produce forests that last.

Equally important is the involvement of local communities. Where residents have secure rights to use mangrove resources sustainably, they have often proved the most effective guardians against illegal cutting. Where they are excluded, the forest becomes, in effect, no one's responsibility. Protection, like restoration, works best when those who live beside the forest have a stake in its survival.

It can be inferred that estimates of the monetary worth of mangroves have been useful mainly because they

- **A.** measure the exact value of every mangrove service  _(error: reversed - estimates are 'imperfect')_
- **B.** make dispersed public benefits visible to decision-makers ✅
- **C.** allow shrimp farms to calculate their profits accurately  _(error: out of scope)_
- **D.** prove that mangroves are more valuable than any other forest  _(error: extreme)_

**Working**

1. Costs of clearing 'appear nowhere'; valuation helps 'persuade governments' by putting a number on them.

**Formula:** An inference must follow necessarily from the text without new assumptions; reject extreme or out-of-scope claims.  
**Trap:** 'Imperfect' rules out 'exact'.

## ENA-095 · L4 · hard · Vocabulary from context within a passage · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

In the passage, 'natural capital' refers to

- **A.** nature treated as an asset that yields benefits ✅
- **B.** funds reserved for tree-planting programmes  _(error: distortion)_
- **C.** the chief city of a coastal administrative region  _(error: wrong sense of 'capital')_
- **D.** money raised by selling off natural resources  _(error: distortion of 'capital' as cash)_

**Working**

1. Mangroves produce 'a stream of benefits' - like capital that yields returns.

**Formula:** Read two sentences either side; choose the meaning the context forces, not the literal or most common sense.  
**Trap:** The economic sense of capital: a stock that yields a flow.
