## ENA-096 · L4 · hard · Argument evaluation in a passage · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

By the most commonly cited measure, financial inclusion in India has advanced at a pace few would have predicted. In little more than a decade, the share of adults with a bank account rose steeply to a large majority, driven by a national campaign to open basic no-frills accounts, the spread of biometric identification and the channelling of government transfers into those accounts. On paper, the unbanked have nearly disappeared.

Ownership of an account, however, is not the same as its use. Surveys have found that a significant share of the new accounts remain dormant, or are used only to withdraw government transfers as soon as they arrive. Many account holders still save in cash, borrow from moneylenders and send money through informal channels. For them, the bank account is a pipe through which money passes, not a place where it stays.

Several reasons explain the gap. The nearest bank branch or business correspondent may be far away or unreliable; transactions may fail because of connectivity problems; and bank staff may treat small customers with impatience. Products are often ill-matched to irregular incomes: a daily-wage labourer cannot commit to a fixed monthly deposit, and a farmer's need for credit follows the crop cycle rather than the bank's calendar. Above all, trust takes longer to build than accounts take to open. A household that has lost money to a failed chit fund or a fraudulent agent will not easily entrust its savings to an institution it barely knows.

None of this means that the account-opening drive was misguided. Accounts are the necessary foundation on which other services, such as savings, insurance, pensions and credit, can be built, and the ability to deliver transfers directly has reduced leakage and delay. The error lies in treating the number of accounts as the measure of success. A target that can be met by opening accounts will be met by opening accounts, whether or not anyone uses them.

The next phase requires different metrics and different efforts: measuring how many people save regularly, obtain credit on fair terms or are protected by insurance, and designing products around the rhythms of their lives. It also requires patience. Inclusion that is counted in accounts can be achieved by a campaign; inclusion that is measured in use must be earned over years, one transaction at a time.

Which of the following, if true, would most strengthen the author's view that dormancy stems partly from products ill-matched to irregular incomes?

- **A.** Most new accounts were opened during a national campaign  _(error: irrelevant - restates background)_
- **B.** Usage rose once flexible small-deposit products were offered ✅
- **C.** Moneylenders charge far higher interest than banks do  _(error: irrelevant - does not address the mismatch)_
- **D.** Government transfers now reach beneficiaries without delay  _(error: irrelevant to product design)_

**Working**

1. If flexible products raise usage, product mismatch was part of the cause.

**Formula:** Isolate the conclusion and its premise; the right option bears directly on the link between them (strengthen / weaken / assume).  
**Trap:** Look for evidence that changing the cause changes the effect.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

The author implies that using the number of accounts as the measure of success

- **A.** was deliberately chosen to hide dormant accounts  _(error: out of scope - no intent is alleged)_
- **B.** should be replaced by counting bank branches  _(error: out of scope)_

**Formula:** An inference must follow necessarily from the text without new assumptions; reject extreme or out-of-scope claims.  
**Trap:** Criticising a metric is not condemning the campaign.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## ENA-099 · L4 · hard · Main idea and central theme · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

By the most commonly cited measure, financial inclusion in India has advanced at a pace few would have predicted. In little more than a decade, the share of adults with a bank account rose steeply to a large majority, driven by a national campaign to open basic no-frills accounts, the spread of biometric identification and the channelling of government transfers into those accounts. On paper, the unbanked have nearly disappeared.

Ownership of an account, however, is not the same as its use. Surveys have found that a significant share of the new accounts remain dormant, or are used only to withdraw government transfers as soon as they arrive. Many account holders still save in cash, borrow from moneylenders and send money through informal channels. For them, the bank account is a pipe through which money passes, not a place where it stays.

Several reasons explain the gap. The nearest bank branch or business correspondent may be far away or unreliable; transactions may fail because of connectivity problems; and bank staff may treat small customers with impatience. Products are often ill-matched to irregular incomes: a daily-wage labourer cannot commit to a fixed monthly deposit, and a farmer's need for credit follows the crop cycle rather than the bank's calendar. Above all, trust takes longer to build than accounts take to open. A household that has lost money to a failed chit fund or a fraudulent agent will not easily entrust its savings to an institution it barely knows.

None of this means that the account-opening drive was misguided. Accounts are the necessary foundation on which other services, such as savings, insurance, pensions and credit, can be built, and the ability to deliver transfers directly has reduced leakage and delay. The error lies in treating the number of accounts as the measure of success. A target that can be met by opening accounts will be met by opening accounts, whether or not anyone uses them.

The next phase requires different metrics and different efforts: measuring how many people save regularly, obtain credit on fair terms or are protected by insurance, and designing products around the rhythms of their lives. It also requires patience. Inclusion that is counted in accounts can be achieved by a campaign; inclusion that is measured in use must be earned over years, one transaction at a time.

Which of the following best expresses the main idea of the passage?

- **A.** The account-opening drive in India was a costly mistake  _(error: reversed)_
- **B.** Inclusion should be judged by account use, not by accounts opened ✅
- **C.** Direct transfers have reduced leakage in welfare delivery  _(error: too narrow - supporting detail)_
- **D.** Most Indians still prefer moneylenders to banks  _(error: extreme and not stated)_

**Working**

1. Accounts rose (1), but use lags (2-3); the drive was right but the metric wrong (4); measure use (5).

**Formula:** The main idea covers the whole passage including the author's conclusion; reject options that are too narrow, too broad or reversed.  
**Trap:** The final two paragraphs carry the thesis.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## ENA-100 · L4 · hard · Vocabulary from context within a passage · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

By the most commonly cited measure, financial inclusion in India has advanced at a pace few would have predicted. In little more than a decade, the share of adults with a bank account rose steeply to a large majority, driven by a national campaign to open basic no-frills accounts, the spread of biometric identification and the channelling of government transfers into those accounts. On paper, the unbanked have nearly disappeared.

Ownership of an account, however, is not the same as its use. Surveys have found that a significant share of the new accounts remain dormant, or are used only to withdraw government transfers as soon as they arrive. Many account holders still save in cash, borrow from moneylenders and send money through informal channels. For them, the bank account is a pipe through which money passes, not a place where it stays.

Several reasons explain the gap. The nearest bank branch or business correspondent may be far away or unreliable; transactions may fail because of connectivity problems; and bank staff may treat small customers with impatience. Products are often ill-matched to irregular incomes: a daily-wage labourer cannot commit to a fixed monthly deposit, and a farmer's need for credit follows the crop cycle rather than the bank's calendar. Above all, trust takes longer to build than accounts take to open. A household that has lost money to a failed chit fund or a fraudulent agent will not easily entrust its savings to an institution it barely knows.

None of this means that the account-opening drive was misguided. Accounts are the necessary foundation on which other services, such as savings, insurance, pensions and credit, can be built, and the ability to deliver transfers directly has reduced leakage and delay. The error lies in treating the number of accounts as the measure of success. A target that can be met by opening accounts will be met by opening accounts, whether or not anyone uses them.

The next phase requires different metrics and different efforts: measuring how many people save regularly, obtain credit on fair terms or are protected by insurance, and designing products around the rhythms of their lives. It also requires patience. Inclusion that is counted in accounts can be achieved by a campaign; inclusion that is measured in use must be earned over years, one transaction at a time.

In the passage, the bank account as 'a pipe through which money passes, not a place where it stays' is one that is

- **A.** a channel for illegal transfers of money  _(error: distortion)_
- **B.** used only as a conduit, not for saving ✅
- **C.** an account that leaks money through fees  _(error: literal misreading of 'pipe')_
- **D.** the main store of the household's savings  _(error: reversed)_

**Working**

1. Transfers are withdrawn 'as soon as they arrive'; savings stay in cash.

**Formula:** Read two sentences either side; choose the meaning the context forces, not the literal or most common sense.  
**Trap:** A pipe carries; it does not hold.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## ENA-101 · L4 · hard · Argument evaluation in a passage · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

Every wave of automation has provoked fears of mass unemployment, and every wave so far has ended with more people employed than before. Mechanised looms displaced hand weavers, tractors displaced farm labourers and computers displaced clerks, yet total employment rose as new industries and occupations absorbed the displaced and as rising productivity lowered prices and raised demand. Optimists conclude that the current wave, driven by artificial intelligence, will follow the same pattern.

The historical record supports their conclusion less strongly than they suggest. Aggregate employment did recover, but the adjustment was frequently long and painful for those who bore it. Many displaced workers never found comparable jobs; the new occupations went to their children, or to younger workers with different skills, in different places. An economy can absorb a technological shock over a generation while the individuals caught in it suffer for the rest of their working lives.

Moreover, there are reasons to think the present wave may differ in one respect. Earlier machines replaced mainly physical and routine tasks, leaving humans with an advantage in work requiring judgement, communication and adaptability. Recent systems encroach on precisely those capabilities: drafting documents, analysing images, answering customers' questions. The tasks that remain distinctively human may be fewer, or may shift more quickly, than in the past. This is not a prediction of mass unemployment, but it is a reason to doubt that the past guarantees a painless future.

What matters, then, is not only how many jobs exist in the end but how the transition is managed. Countries differ greatly in this respect. Where retraining programmes are closely linked to employers' needs, where unemployment support is adequate and portable, and where workers have a voice in how technology is introduced, displaced workers move to new jobs faster and with smaller losses. Where such institutions are weak, the gains from automation flow disproportionately to the owners of capital and to highly skilled workers, and inequality widens even as average incomes rise.

For a country like India, with a young and growing workforce and a large informal sector, the stakes are particularly high. Automation could raise productivity in manufacturing and services, but it could also narrow the path by which millions of workers have traditionally moved from farms into factories and offices. The question is not whether to adopt new technology, since refusing it would merely cede ground to competitors, but whether institutions for training, social protection and bargaining can be built quickly enough to spread its benefits widely.

Which of the following, if true, would most weaken the author's suggestion that the present wave of automation may differ from earlier ones?

- **A.** Retraining works best when linked to employers' needs  _(error: irrelevant - about managing transition)_
- **B.** Mechanised looms displaced large numbers of hand weavers  _(error: restates history, no bearing)_
- **C.** AI still fails at most tasks needing judgement and dialogue ✅
- **D.** India's workforce is young and growing rapidly  _(error: irrelevant)_

**Working**

1. The difference claimed is that AI encroaches on judgement and communication; if it largely fails there, the difference disappears.

**Formula:** Isolate the conclusion and its premise; the right option bears directly on the link between them (strengthen / weaken / assume).  
**Trap:** Attack the specific premise of the claim.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## ENA-102 · L4 · hard · Author tone, attitude and purpose · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

Every wave of automation has provoked fears of mass unemployment, and every wave so far has ended with more people employed than before. Mechanised looms displaced hand weavers, tractors displaced farm labourers and computers displaced clerks, yet total employment rose as new industries and occupations absorbed the displaced and as rising productivity lowered prices and raised demand. Optimists conclude that the current wave, driven by artificial intelligence, will follow the same pattern.

The historical record supports their conclusion less strongly than they suggest. Aggregate employment did recover, but the adjustment was frequently long and painful for those who bore it. Many displaced workers never found comparable jobs; the new occupations went to their children, or to younger workers with different skills, in different places. An economy can absorb a technological shock over a generation while the individuals caught in it suffer for the rest of their working lives.

Moreover, there are reasons to think the present wave may differ in one respect. Earlier machines replaced mainly physical and routine tasks, leaving humans with an advantage in work requiring judgement, communication and adaptability. Recent systems encroach on precisely those capabilities: drafting documents, analysing images, answering customers' questions. The tasks that remain distinctively human may be fewer, or may shift more quickly, than in the past. This is not a prediction of mass unemployment, but it is a reason to doubt that the past guarantees a painless future.

What matters, then, is not only how many jobs exist in the end but how the transition is managed. Countries differ greatly in this respect. Where retraining programmes are closely linked to employers' needs, where unemployment support is adequate and portable, and where workers have a voice in how technology is introduced, displaced workers move to new jobs faster and with smaller losses. Where such institutions are weak, the gains from automation flow disproportionately to the owners of capital and to highly skilled workers, and inequality widens even as average incomes rise.

For a country like India, with a young and growing workforce and a large informal sector, the stakes are particularly high. Automation could raise productivity in manufacturing and services, but it could also narrow the path by which millions of workers have traditionally moved from farms into factories and offices. The question is not whether to adopt new technology, since refusing it would merely cede ground to competitors, but whether institutions for training, social protection and bargaining can be built quickly enough to spread its benefits widely.

The author's attitude towards the optimists' conclusion is one of

- **A.** scornful rejection  _(error: extreme - the author accepts that employment recovered)_
- **B.** qualified disagreement ✅
- **C.** wholehearted endorsement  _(error: reversed)_
- **D.** indifference  _(error: wrong)_

**Working**

1. 'The historical record supports their conclusion less strongly than they suggest' - partial acceptance, partial rejection.

**Formula:** Judge tone from the author's own evaluative words and qualifiers, not from views the author merely reports.  
**Trap:** Conceding aggregate recovery rules out scorn.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## ENA-103 · L4 · hard · Inference and implied meaning · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

Every wave of automation has provoked fears of mass unemployment, and every wave so far has ended with more people employed than before. Mechanised looms displaced hand weavers, tractors displaced farm labourers and computers displaced clerks, yet total employment rose as new industries and occupations absorbed the displaced and as rising productivity lowered prices and raised demand. Optimists conclude that the current wave, driven by artificial intelligence, will follow the same pattern.

The historical record supports their conclusion less strongly than they suggest. Aggregate employment did recover, but the adjustment was frequently long and painful for those who bore it. Many displaced workers never found comparable jobs; the new occupations went to their children, or to younger workers with different skills, in different places. An economy can absorb a technological shock over a generation while the individuals caught in it suffer for the rest of their working lives.

Moreover, there are reasons to think the present wave may differ in one respect. Earlier machines replaced mainly physical and routine tasks, leaving humans with an advantage in work requiring judgement, communication and adaptability. Recent systems encroach on precisely those capabilities: drafting documents, analysing images, answering customers' questions. The tasks that remain distinctively human may be fewer, or may shift more quickly, than in the past. This is not a prediction of mass unemployment, but it is a reason to doubt that the past guarantees a painless future.

What matters, then, is not only how many jobs exist in the end but how the transition is managed. Countries differ greatly in this respect. Where retraining programmes are closely linked to employers' needs, where unemployment support is adequate and portable, and where workers have a voice in how technology is introduced, displaced workers move to new jobs faster and with smaller losses. Where such institutions are weak, the gains from automation flow disproportionately to the owners of capital and to highly skilled workers, and inequality widens even as average incomes rise.

For a country like India, with a young and growing workforce and a large informal sector, the stakes are particularly high. Automation could raise productivity in manufacturing and services, but it could also narrow the path by which millions of workers have traditionally moved from farms into factories and offices. The question is not whether to adopt new technology, since refusing it would merely cede ground to competitors, but whether institutions for training, social protection and bargaining can be built quickly enough to spread its benefits widely.

It can be inferred that the author regards rising average incomes as

- **A.** proof that automation benefits all workers equally  _(error: reversed - inequality can widen)_
- **B.** an incomplete measure of how well a transition is managed ✅
- **C.** unlikely to occur with the spread of automation  _(error: reversed - average incomes may rise)_
- **D.** the chief goal of retraining programmes  _(error: out of scope)_

**Working**

1. 'Inequality widens even as average incomes rise' - averages hide distribution.

**Formula:** An inference must follow necessarily from the text without new assumptions; reject extreme or out-of-scope claims.  
**Trap:** Averages can rise while many lose.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## ENA-104 · L4 · hard · Main idea and central theme · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

Every wave of automation has provoked fears of mass unemployment, and every wave so far has ended with more people employed than before. Mechanised looms displaced hand weavers, tractors displaced farm labourers and computers displaced clerks, yet total employment rose as new industries and occupations absorbed the displaced and as rising productivity lowered prices and raised demand. Optimists conclude that the current wave, driven by artificial intelligence, will follow the same pattern.

The historical record supports their conclusion less strongly than they suggest. Aggregate employment did recover, but the adjustment was frequently long and painful for those who bore it. Many displaced workers never found comparable jobs; the new occupations went to their children, or to younger workers with different skills, in different places. An economy can absorb a technological shock over a generation while the individuals caught in it suffer for the rest of their working lives.

Moreover, there are reasons to think the present wave may differ in one respect. Earlier machines replaced mainly physical and routine tasks, leaving humans with an advantage in work requiring judgement, communication and adaptability. Recent systems encroach on precisely those capabilities: drafting documents, analysing images, answering customers' questions. The tasks that remain distinctively human may be fewer, or may shift more quickly, than in the past. This is not a prediction of mass unemployment, but it is a reason to doubt that the past guarantees a painless future.

What matters, then, is not only how many jobs exist in the end but how the transition is managed. Countries differ greatly in this respect. Where retraining programmes are closely linked to employers' needs, where unemployment support is adequate and portable, and where workers have a voice in how technology is introduced, displaced workers move to new jobs faster and with smaller losses. Where such institutions are weak, the gains from automation flow disproportionately to the owners of capital and to highly skilled workers, and inequality widens even as average incomes rise.

For a country like India, with a young and growing workforce and a large informal sector, the stakes are particularly high. Automation could raise productivity in manufacturing and services, but it could also narrow the path by which millions of workers have traditionally moved from farms into factories and offices. The question is not whether to adopt new technology, since refusing it would merely cede ground to competitors, but whether institutions for training, social protection and bargaining can be built quickly enough to spread its benefits widely.

Which of the following best states the main idea of the passage?

- **A.** Institutions managing the transition decide automation's impact ✅
- **B.** India should restrict the adoption of new technology  _(error: reversed - refusing would 'cede ground')_
- **C.** History proves that automation always creates more jobs  _(error: the optimists' view, which the author qualifies)_
- **D.** Artificial intelligence will cause mass unemployment in India  _(error: reversed - 'not a prediction of mass unemployment')_

**Working**

1. Paras 2-5 shift attention from job totals to how the transition is managed and by what institutions.

**Formula:** The main idea covers the whole passage including the author's conclusion; reject options that are too narrow, too broad or reversed.  
**Trap:** The author's thesis is about institutions, not job counts.

## ENA-105 · L4 · hard · Passage-based synonym and antonym · officer

Read the passage and answer the question that follows.

Every wave of automation has provoked fears of mass unemployment, and every wave so far has ended with more people employed than before. Mechanised looms displaced hand weavers, tractors displaced farm labourers and computers displaced clerks, yet total employment rose as new industries and occupations absorbed the displaced and as rising productivity lowered prices and raised demand. Optimists conclude that the current wave, driven by artificial intelligence, will follow the same pattern.

The historical record supports their conclusion less strongly than they suggest. Aggregate employment did recover, but the adjustment was frequently long and painful for those who bore it. Many displaced workers never found comparable jobs; the new occupations went to their children, or to younger workers with different skills, in different places. An economy can absorb a technological shock over a generation while the individuals caught in it suffer for the rest of their working lives.

Moreover, there are reasons to think the present wave may differ in one respect. Earlier machines replaced mainly physical and routine tasks, leaving humans with an advantage in work requiring judgement, communication and adaptability. Recent systems encroach on precisely those capabilities: drafting documents, analysing images, answering customers' questions. The tasks that remain distinctively human may be fewer, or may shift more quickly, than in the past. This is not a prediction of mass unemployment, but it is a reason to doubt that the past guarantees a painless future.

What matters, then, is not only how many jobs exist in the end but how the transition is managed. Countries differ greatly in this respect. Where retraining programmes are closely linked to employers' needs, where unemployment support is adequate and portable, and where workers have a voice in how technology is introduced, displaced workers move to new jobs faster and with smaller losses. Where such institutions are weak, the gains from automation flow disproportionately to the owners of capital and to highly skilled workers, and inequality widens even as average incomes rise.

For a country like India, with a young and growing workforce and a large informal sector, the stakes are particularly high. Automation could raise productivity in manufacturing and services, but it could also narrow the path by which millions of workers have traditionally moved from farms into factories and offices. The question is not whether to adopt new technology, since refusing it would merely cede ground to competitors, but whether institutions for training, social protection and bargaining can be built quickly enough to spread its benefits widely.

Which word is closest in meaning to 'encroach' as used in the passage?

- **A.** depend  _(error: unrelated meaning)_
- **B.** improve  _(error: unrelated meaning)_
- **C.** retreat  _(error: antonym)_
- **D.** intrude ✅

**Working**

1. Recent systems 'encroach on' human capabilities - advance into territory once held by humans.

**Formula:** Replace the word with each option in its sentence; keep the sense the passage uses, not a different dictionary sense.  
**Trap:** Substitute: 'intrude on precisely those capabilities'.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## ENA-106 · L1 · easy · Connector and transition cloze · foundation

Choose the most appropriate connector for the blank.

The monsoon arrived two weeks late this year. ______, farmers delayed sowing their kharif crops.

- **A.** Similarly  _(error: comparison signal - no parallel is drawn)_
- **B.** Otherwise  _(error: condition signal - does not fit a stated fact)_
- **C.** Nevertheless  _(error: contrast signal - the second sentence is a result, not a contrast)_
- **D.** Consequently ✅

**Working**

1. Late monsoon (cause) -> delayed sowing (effect): a result connector is needed.

**Formula:** Identify the logical relation between the two ideas (contrast, cause, addition, example, condition, sequence) and pick the connector that signals it.  
**Trap:** Name the relation before reading the options.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## ENA-107 · L1 · easy · Connector and transition cloze · foundation

Choose the most appropriate connector for the blank.

The bank reported record profits this year. ______, its share price fell because investors were worried about rising bad loans.

- **A.** Therefore  _(error: cause-effect signal - record profits do not cause a price fall)_
- **B.** Nevertheless ✅
- **C.** Likewise  _(error: comparison signal - the ideas are opposed, not similar)_
- **D.** For instance  _(error: example signal - the second sentence is not an example)_

**Working**

1. Good news (profits) followed by an unexpected outcome (price fell) needs a contrast connector.

**Formula:** Identify the logical relation between the two ideas (contrast, cause, addition, example, condition, sequence) and pick the connector that signals it.  
**Trap:** An unexpected outcome signals contrast.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## ENA-108 · L1 · easy · Connector and transition cloze · foundation

Choose the most appropriate connector for the blank.

Many small shops now accept digital payments; ______, a vegetable vendor near my house accepts payment only through a QR code.

- **A.** nonetheless  _(error: concession signal - nothing is being conceded)_
- **B.** for instance ✅
- **C.** otherwise  _(error: condition signal - grammatical and logical misfit)_
- **D.** in contrast  _(error: contrast signal - the vendor illustrates the trend)_

**Working**

1. The vendor is a specific example of the general statement.

**Formula:** Identify the logical relation between the two ideas (contrast, cause, addition, example, condition, sequence) and pick the connector that signals it.  
**Trap:** General claim followed by a specific case = example.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## ENA-109 · L1 · easy · Connector and transition cloze · foundation

Choose the most appropriate connector for the blank.

You must link your mobile number to your bank account; ______, you will not receive transaction alerts.

- **A.** moreover  _(error: addition signal - the clause states a consequence of not acting)_
- **B.** otherwise ✅
- **C.** similarly  _(error: comparison signal)_
- **D.** therefore  _(error: result signal - linking does not cause missing alerts)_

**Working**

1. 'Otherwise' = if you do not do this; not linking -> no alerts.

**Formula:** Identify the logical relation between the two ideas (contrast, cause, addition, example, condition, sequence) and pick the connector that signals it.  
**Trap:** A warning of what happens if an instruction is ignored takes 'otherwise'.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## ENA-110 · L2 · medium · Connector and transition cloze · foundation

Choose the most appropriate connector for the blank.

The committee studied the proposal for three months. ______, it recommended that the project be dropped.

- **A.** Conversely  _(error: opposite-relation signal - misfit)_
- **B.** Eventually ✅
- **C.** Instead  _(error: substitution signal - nothing is being replaced)_
- **D.** Meanwhile  _(error: simultaneity signal - the recommendation came after the study)_

**Working**

1. The recommendation came at the end of a long process - a sequence/time connector.

**Formula:** Identify the logical relation between the two ideas (contrast, cause, addition, example, condition, sequence) and pick the connector that signals it.  
**Trap:** 'Meanwhile' means at the same time.

## ENA-111 · L4 · hard · Connector and transition cloze · officer

In the passage below, some blanks are numbered. Read the passage and choose the most appropriate option for the blank asked about.

India has added solar capacity at a remarkable pace over the past decade. (1) ______, the sun does not shine at night, and the output of solar parks falls sharply during the monsoon. Grid operators must therefore keep other power sources ready to fill the gap, and until recently that role fell almost entirely to coal plants, which cannot be switched on and off quickly. (2) ______, the more solar power the grid absorbs, the harder it becomes to balance supply and demand from hour to hour.

Battery storage offers one answer. Batteries can soak up surplus solar power at midday and release it after sunset. (3) ______ their costs have fallen steeply, they remain expensive for storing energy over many hours, let alone across seasons. Pumped hydro storage, which uses surplus power to lift water to an upper reservoir and releases it through turbines when needed, can store larger quantities for longer. (4) ______, it requires suitable terrain and years of construction.

No single technology, then, will solve the problem. A combination of batteries for short gaps, pumped hydro for longer ones, flexible gas plants and better forecasting of demand appears most promising. Stronger links between regional grids can also help, since the sun may be shining in one region while clouds cover another. (5) ______ such measures are put in place, further additions of solar capacity risk being wasted at midday and unavailable when they are most needed.

Which of the following best fills blank (1)?

- **A.** For example  _(error: example signal - not an illustration of rapid growth)_
- **B.** Therefore  _(error: cause-effect signal - rapid growth does not cause darkness at night)_
- **C.** Similarly  _(error: comparison signal - the sentence introduces a limitation)_
- **D.** However ✅

**Working**

1. Achievement (rapid addition) followed by a limitation (no sun at night) = contrast.

**Formula:** Identify the logical relation between the two ideas (contrast, cause, addition, example, condition, sequence) and pick the connector that signals it.  
**Trap:** A drawback after an achievement signals contrast.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## ENA-112 · L4 · hard · Connector and transition cloze · officer

In the passage below, some blanks are numbered. Read the passage and choose the most appropriate option for the blank asked about.

India has added solar capacity at a remarkable pace over the past decade. (1) ______, the sun does not shine at night, and the output of solar parks falls sharply during the monsoon. Grid operators must therefore keep other power sources ready to fill the gap, and until recently that role fell almost entirely to coal plants, which cannot be switched on and off quickly. (2) ______, the more solar power the grid absorbs, the harder it becomes to balance supply and demand from hour to hour.

Battery storage offers one answer. Batteries can soak up surplus solar power at midday and release it after sunset. (3) ______ their costs have fallen steeply, they remain expensive for storing energy over many hours, let alone across seasons. Pumped hydro storage, which uses surplus power to lift water to an upper reservoir and releases it through turbines when needed, can store larger quantities for longer. (4) ______, it requires suitable terrain and years of construction.

No single technology, then, will solve the problem. A combination of batteries for short gaps, pumped hydro for longer ones, flexible gas plants and better forecasting of demand appears most promising. Stronger links between regional grids can also help, since the sun may be shining in one region while clouds cover another. (5) ______ such measures are put in place, further additions of solar capacity risk being wasted at midday and unavailable when they are most needed.

Which of the following best fills blank (2)?

- **A.** In contrast  _(error: contrast signal - misfit)_
- **B.** Unless  _(error: subordinating conjunction - cannot begin a sentence followed only by a comma and a main clause here)_
- **C.** Consequently ✅
- **D.** Nevertheless  _(error: contrast signal - the sentence follows from the previous facts)_

**Working**

1. Intermittent solar + inflexible coal backup -> more solar makes balancing harder: a result.

**Formula:** Identify the logical relation between the two ideas (contrast, cause, addition, example, condition, sequence) and pick the connector that signals it.  
**Trap:** Check grammar as well as logic: 'Unless' needs its own clause.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

Which of the following best fills blank (3)?

- **A.** Unless  _(error: condition signal - meaning misfit)_
- **B.** Because  _(error: causal signal - falling costs do not cause expense)_
- **C.** However  _(error: conjunctive adverb - cannot introduce a subordinate clause joined to the main clause by a comma)_
- **D.** Although ✅

**Working**

1. Concession: costs have fallen, yet batteries remain expensive for long storage.

**Formula:** Identify the logical relation between the two ideas (contrast, cause, addition, example, condition, sequence) and pick the connector that signals it.  
**Trap:** 'However' cannot do the job of 'although' inside one sentence.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## ENA-114 · L4 · hard · Connector and transition cloze · officer

In the passage below, some blanks are numbered. Read the passage and choose the most appropriate option for the blank asked about.

India has added solar capacity at a remarkable pace over the past decade. (1) ______, the sun does not shine at night, and the output of solar parks falls sharply during the monsoon. Grid operators must therefore keep other power sources ready to fill the gap, and until recently that role fell almost entirely to coal plants, which cannot be switched on and off quickly. (2) ______, the more solar power the grid absorbs, the harder it becomes to balance supply and demand from hour to hour.

Battery storage offers one answer. Batteries can soak up surplus solar power at midday and release it after sunset. (3) ______ their costs have fallen steeply, they remain expensive for storing energy over many hours, let alone across seasons. Pumped hydro storage, which uses surplus power to lift water to an upper reservoir and releases it through turbines when needed, can store larger quantities for longer. (4) ______, it requires suitable terrain and years of construction.

No single technology, then, will solve the problem. A combination of batteries for short gaps, pumped hydro for longer ones, flexible gas plants and better forecasting of demand appears most promising. Stronger links between regional grids can also help, since the sun may be shining in one region while clouds cover another. (5) ______ such measures are put in place, further additions of solar capacity risk being wasted at midday and unavailable when they are most needed.

Which of the following best fills blank (4)?

- **A.** Moreover  _(error: addition signal - the sentence gives a drawback, not a further advantage)_
- **B.** For instance  _(error: example signal)_
- **C.** On the other hand ✅
- **D.** Hence  _(error: result signal - terrain needs are not a result of storage capacity)_

**Working**

1. Advantage of pumped hydro (larger, longer storage) set against its drawback (terrain, time).

**Formula:** Identify the logical relation between the two ideas (contrast, cause, addition, example, condition, sequence) and pick the connector that signals it.  
**Trap:** Advantage vs drawback = contrast.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## ENA-115 · L4 · hard · Connector and transition cloze · officer

In the passage below, some blanks are numbered. Read the passage and choose the most appropriate option for the blank asked about.

India has added solar capacity at a remarkable pace over the past decade. (1) ______, the sun does not shine at night, and the output of solar parks falls sharply during the monsoon. Grid operators must therefore keep other power sources ready to fill the gap, and until recently that role fell almost entirely to coal plants, which cannot be switched on and off quickly. (2) ______, the more solar power the grid absorbs, the harder it becomes to balance supply and demand from hour to hour.

Battery storage offers one answer. Batteries can soak up surplus solar power at midday and release it after sunset. (3) ______ their costs have fallen steeply, they remain expensive for storing energy over many hours, let alone across seasons. Pumped hydro storage, which uses surplus power to lift water to an upper reservoir and releases it through turbines when needed, can store larger quantities for longer. (4) ______, it requires suitable terrain and years of construction.

No single technology, then, will solve the problem. A combination of batteries for short gaps, pumped hydro for longer ones, flexible gas plants and better forecasting of demand appears most promising. Stronger links between regional grids can also help, since the sun may be shining in one region while clouds cover another. (5) ______ such measures are put in place, further additions of solar capacity risk being wasted at midday and unavailable when they are most needed.

Which of the following best fills blank (5)?

- **A.** Unless ✅
- **B.** Once  _(error: reversed - once measures are in place, the risk would fall, not arise)_
- **C.** Because  _(error: reversed - the measures would prevent waste, not cause it)_
- **D.** Although  _(error: concession signal - logical misfit)_

**Working**

1. The risk of waste arises if the measures are NOT in place - a negative condition.

**Formula:** Identify the logical relation between the two ideas (contrast, cause, addition, example, condition, sequence) and pick the connector that signals it.  
**Trap:** Test the full sentence with each option.

## ENA-116 · L4 · hard · Connector and transition cloze · officer

In the passage below, some blanks are numbered. Read the passage and choose the most appropriate option for the blank asked about.

Mergers among public sector banks are usually justified by the promise of scale. A larger bank, the argument runs, can spread the cost of technology over more customers, lend to bigger projects and compete more effectively with private and foreign rivals. (1) ______, the evidence from past mergers is mixed. Some merged banks did become stronger; others spent years reconciling incompatible computer systems, rival staff hierarchies and different lending cultures.

The difficulty lies not in the arithmetic of combining balance sheets but in the human work of combining institutions. Employees who fear transfers or lost promotions may resist change, (2) ______ customers may drift away if branches close or services are disrupted during the transition. (3) ______ a merger is carefully planned, these frictions can consume the very efficiencies it was meant to deliver.

Scale, moreover, is not a cure for weak governance. A bank that lent carelessly before a merger will not lend prudently after it simply because it has become larger. (4) ______, combining a weak bank with a strong one can divert the strong bank's management attention and burden it with bad loans. Mergers can be useful, but only as part of a broader reform of how banks are governed and supervised. (5) ______ that reform, consolidation merely produces fewer, larger banks with the same old problems.

Which of the following best fills blank (1)?

- **A.** For this reason  _(error: cause signal - misfit)_
- **B.** Yet ✅
- **C.** Therefore  _(error: cause-effect signal - the promise of scale does not produce mixed evidence)_
- **D.** Likewise  _(error: comparison signal)_

**Working**

1. The promised benefits are set against mixed evidence - contrast.

**Formula:** Identify the logical relation between the two ideas (contrast, cause, addition, example, condition, sequence) and pick the connector that signals it.  
**Trap:** Claim followed by counter-evidence = contrast.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

In the passage below, some blanks are numbered. Read the passage and choose the most appropriate option for the blank asked about.

Mergers among public sector banks are usually justified by the promise of scale. The difficulty lies not in the arithmetic of combining balance sheets but in the human work of combining institutions. Employees who fear transfers or lost promotions may resist change, (2) ______ customers may drift away if branches close or services are disrupted during the transition. Scale, moreover, is not a cure for weak governance. A bank that lent carelessly before a merger will not lend prudently after it simply because it has become larger. In the passage below, some blanks are numbered. Read the passage and choose the most appropriate option for the blank asked about.

Mergers among public sector banks are usually justified by the promise of scale. The difficulty lies not in the arithmetic of combining balance sheets but in the human work of combining institutions. Scale, moreover, is not a cure for weak governance. A bank that lent carelessly before a merger will not lend prudently after it simply because it has become larger. Mergers can be useful, but only as part of a broader reform of how banks are governed and supervised. ## ENA-121 · L1 · easy · Double-blank sentence completion · foundation

Choose the pair of words that best fills the two blanks in the sentence, in order.

The minister ______ the new scheme at a function attended by thousands of farmers, who ______ it warmly.

- **A.** launched, opposed  _(error: collocation error - 'opposed it warmly' is contradictory)_
- **B.** cancelled, welcomed  _(error: logic - farmers would not warmly welcome a cancellation announced at a launch-type function)_
- **C.** launched, welcomed ✅
- **D.** postponed, criticised  _(error: collocation error - 'criticised it warmly')_

**Working**

1. 'Warmly' requires a positive response; a function signals a launch.

**Formula:** Fix the logical signal (though / because / far from / rather than) first; both words must fit it and each other.  
**Trap:** Adverb 'warmly' decides the second blank.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

1. 'Not only ... but also'; exercise lowers stress.

**Formula:** Fix the logical signal (though / because / far from / rather than) first; both words must fit it and each other.

## ENA-136 · L1 · easy · Multi-blank passage cloze · foundation

In the passage below, some blanks are numbered. Read the passage and choose the most appropriate option for the blank asked about.

Ten years ago, the village of Kheda faced a (1) ______ water shortage every summer. Wells ran dry by April, and women walked several kilometres to fetch water. Then the villagers decided to (2) ______ matters into their own hands. With help from a local organisation, they built small check dams across the stream that flows past the village and (3) ______ old ponds that had filled with silt. The work was done mostly by the villagers themselves, who (4) ______ their labour free of cost. Within three years, the water level in the wells had risen noticeably, and farmers were able to grow a second crop. Kheda's experience shows that communities can (5) ______ even serious problems when they work together.

Which of the following best fills blank (1)?

- **A.** strict  _(error: collocation error - 'strict shortage')_
- **B.** wide  _(error: collocation error - 'wide shortage')_
- **C.** tall  _(error: collocation error)_
- **D.** severe ✅

**Working**

1. 'Severe shortage' is the standard collocation.

## ENA-141 · L4 · hard · Multi-blank passage cloze · officer

In the passage below, some blanks are numbered. Read the passage and choose the most appropriate option for the blank asked about.

The rapid growth of digital lending apps has brought credit within reach of millions who were once (1) ______ by banks. A salaried worker can now obtain a small loan within minutes, without visiting a branch or submitting a pile of documents. Yet the same speed that makes these apps attractive has also made them fertile ground for (2) ______. Some unregulated apps have charged exorbitant fees hidden in fine print, accessed borrowers' phone contacts without meaningful consent and resorted to harassment to (3) ______ their dues.

Regulators have responded by insisting that loans be disbursed directly into borrowers' bank accounts and that all charges be disclosed upfront as an annual percentage rate. Such rules aim not to (4) ______ digital lending but to ensure that its benefits are not purchased at the cost of borrowers' dignity and privacy. The challenge lies in enforcement: apps can be launched and withdrawn faster than authorities can (5) ______ them, and many operate from outside the country.

Which of the following best fills blank (1)?

- **A.** brought up  _(error: phrasal-verb misfit)_
- **B.** taken in  _(error: meaning misfit - 'taken in' means deceived or admitted)_
- **C.** turned away ✅
- **D.** looked after  _(error: reversed - banks did not serve them)_

**Working**

1. People once refused by banks now get credit - 'turned away'.

**Formula:** Read the whole passage first; each blank must fit grammar, collocation and the passage's line of argument.  
**Trap:** The contrast with 'within reach' decides it.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

- **B.** replaced ✅
- **C.** rejected  _(error: meaning misfit)_
- **D.** resembled  _(error: meaning misfit)_

**Working**

1. Cheap, light plastic has taken the place of glass, paper and metal.

**Formula:** Read the whole passage first; each blank must fit grammar, collocation and the passage's line of argument.  
**Trap:** 'In countless uses' implies substitution.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

**Formula:** Find the context clue (cause, contrast, restatement) that decides the blank.
